Linear inviscid damping for stably stratified Boussinesq flows
Alberto Enciso, Marc Nualart

Abstract
. We study the linear asymptotic stability of stably stratified monotone shear flows for the Boussinesq equations in the periodic channel. By means of the limiting absorption principle, we obtain a precise description of the inviscid damping experienced by the perturbed velocity field and density, with time-decay rates that depend on the local Richardson number 𝒥 ⁡ ( y ) {\mathcal{J}}(y) and split into four stratification regimes (non-stratified, weak, mild, and strong) reflecting qualitative changes in the structure of the Green’s function at the critical thresholds 𝒥 ⁡ ( y ) = 0 {\mathcal{J}}(y)=0 and 𝒥 ⁡ ( y ) = 1 4 {\mathcal{J}}(y)=\frac{1}{4} . The velocity and density decay estimates are later used to prove quantitative sub-linear growth of the vorticity and gradient of density. As a byproduct of our analysis, we show that, under mild hypotheses on the underlying shear-type equilibrium, the spectrum of the linearised Boussinesq operator is purely continuous.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
35Q31, 76B70, 35P05, 76E05

1.
Introduction

Our objective in this paper is to prove linear inviscid damping for the two-dimensional Boussinesq equations on a periodic channel, linearised around a monotone, stably stratified shear flow
\bar{\boldsymbol{v}}=(v(y),0)
,
\bar{\rho}=\bar{\rho}(y)
. Intuitively, the monotonicity condition (
v^{\prime}(y)>0
) serves to ensure sufficiently strong mixing, while the stable stratification condition (
\bar{\rho}^{\prime}(y)\leq 0
, so that lighter fluid lies above heavier fluid) ensures that the potential energy resists vertical displacement.

We provide decay estimates for the perturbations of the velocity
\boldsymbol{v}=(v_{x},v_{y})
and density
\bar{\rho}
, with the key property that the time-decay rates depend on the local Richardson number
{\mathcal{J}}(y)=-\mathfrak{g}\bar{\rho}^{\prime}(y)/[v’(y)]^{2}
, where
\mathfrak{g}>0
is the gravity constant. Specifically, in the non-stratified region (
{\mathcal{J}}(y)=0
), the system reduces to the 2D Euler equations, and we recover the classical decay rates
\|v_{x}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}+t\|v_{y}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim t^{-1}
. In the weakly stratified regions (
0<{\mathcal{J}}(y)<\frac{1}{4}
), the decay is slower:
\|v_{x}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}+t\|v_{y}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim t^{-\frac{1}{2}+[{\frac{1}{4}-{\mathcal{J}}(y)}]^{1/2}}
, and logarithmic losses appear near the critical threshold
{\mathcal{J}}(y)\approx\frac{1}{4}
. In strongly stratified regions (
{\mathcal{J}}(y)>\frac{1}{4}
), one obtains the rate
\|v_{x}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}+t\|v_{y}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim t^{-1/2}
. These four regimes (which recover the expected rates provided by a back-of-the-envelope heuristic calculation) thus provide a unified quantitative description of how stable stratification modulates inviscid damping at the linearized level. Perturbations of the density
\bar{\rho}
also vanish in time, with the decay rates of
\|v_{x}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}
.

1.1.
The Boussinesq equations

The Boussinesq equations model the evolution of an incompressible fluid with small density variations. On the periodic channel
{\mathbb{T}}\times[0,2]
, the equations read as

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}\cdot\nabla)\tilde{\omega}

\displaystyle=-\mathfrak{g}\partial_{x}\tilde{\rho},

(1.1)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}\cdot\nabla)\tilde{\rho}

\displaystyle=0,

where
\mathfrak{g}>0
is the gravity constant.
The unknowns are the velocity field of the fluid,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}:[0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{T}}\times[0,2]\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and its density
\tilde{\rho}:[0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{T}}\times[0,2]
, which appears in the equations as an active scalar that influences the velocity field through a buoyancy force. As the fluid is incompressible, we write the velocity field as
\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}=\nabla^{\perp}\Delta^{-1}\tilde{\omega}
, where
\tilde{\omega}=\nabla^{\perp}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}:[0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{T}}\times[0,2]
is the vorticity. Here and in what follows,
\Delta^{-1}
denotes the inverse of the Laplacian on
{\mathbb{T}}\times[0,2]
with Dirichlet boundary conditions and
\nabla^{\perp}=(-\partial_{y},\partial_{x})
.

Let us now consider the equilibrium solution

\bar{\boldsymbol{v}}=(v(y),0),\qquad\bar{\rho}=\bar{\rho}(y),

(1.2)

with
(x,y)\in[0,2]\times{\mathbb{T}}
, which describes a shear flow velocity field transporting a density stratified in the vertical direction. To understand the long-time dynamics of the equation near this equilibrium, we introduce the perturbed velocity

\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}=\bar{\boldsymbol{v}}+\boldsymbol{v}
and density profile
\tilde{\rho}=\bar{\rho}+\rho
, and define the corresponding vorticity perturbation
{\omega}=\nabla^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}
.

The linearised Euler–Boussinesq system (
1.1
) around the equilibrium (
1.2
) can then be written as

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\omega+v(y)\partial_{x}\omega-v^{\prime\prime}(y)\partial_{x}\psi=-\mathfrak{g}\partial_{x}\rho\\
\partial_{t}\rho+v(y)\partial_{x}\rho=-\partial_{y}\bar{\rho}\partial_{x}\psi,\\
{\Delta}\psi=\omega,\end{cases}

(1.3)

where
\psi=\Delta^{-1}\nabla^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}
is the stream-function of
\boldsymbol{v}
, which satisfies
\psi(x,0)=\psi(x,2)=0
. The linearised system can be further expressed in the more compact formulation

\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\omega\\
\rho\end{pmatrix}+{\mathcal{L}}\begin{pmatrix}\omega\\
\rho\end{pmatrix}=0,\quad\begin{pmatrix}\omega\\
\rho\end{pmatrix}\Big|_{t=0}=\begin{pmatrix}\omega^{0}\\
\rho^{0}\end{pmatrix}

(1.4)

in terms of the matrix linear operator

{\mathcal{L}}=\begin{pmatrix}\left(v(y)-v^{\prime\prime}(y){\Delta}^{-1}\right)\partial_{x}&\mathfrak{g}\partial_{x}\\
\partial_{y}\bar{\rho}{\Delta}^{-1}\partial_{x}&v(y)\partial_{x}\end{pmatrix}.

(1.5)

The asymptotic stability of the Boussinesq equations (
1.1
) near stably stratified monotonic shear flows began with the works of Howard
[
Howard
]
and Miles
[
Miles
]
, who addressed the spectral stability of the
constantly stratified Couette flow
, which is given by

\displaystyle\overline{v}_{\mathrm{C}}=(y,0),\qquad\overline{\rho}_{\mathrm{C}}=1-\beta^{2}y/\mathfrak{g}.

(1.6)

Their celebrated Miles–Howard stability criterion ensures the absence of unstable eigenvalues of the linearised operator
{\mathcal{L}}
if
\beta^{2}>\frac{1}{4}
throughout the fluid domain, while such eigenvalues may appear
[
drazin1958stability
]
for
\beta^{2}<\frac{1}{4}
. In the physical literature, asymptotic stability was extensively studied in
[
brown1980algebraic
,
case1960stability
,
chimonas1979algebraic
,
dikii1960stability
,
farrell1993transient
,
hoiland1953dynamic
,
kuo1963perturbations
]
, albeit with disparate conclusions. Later, Hartman
[
Hartman
]
solved (
1.1
) in the periodic channel
{\mathbb{T}}\times{\mathbb{R}}
nearby (
1.6
), and conjectured that the vorticity should be unstable in
L^{2}
, growing as
\sqrt{t}
for
\beta^{2}>\frac{1}{4}
.

Hartman’s approach was mathematically justified in the recent paper
[
YL18
]
, which proves linear inviscid damping of density and velocity fields close to (
1.6
). There, the authors use Fourier analysis and hypergeometric functions to show that the time-decay rates obtained depend on the strength of the stratification
\beta^{2}
: they are uniform for
\beta^{2}>\frac{1}{4}
, experience a logarithmic loss for
\beta^{2}=\frac{1}{4}
, and are an affine function of
\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}-\beta^{2}}
for
\beta^{2}\in(0,\frac{1}{4})
, degenerating as
\beta^{2}\rightarrow 0
.

Shortly afterwards, a variational–energetic method was introduced in
[
BCZD22
]
to address the same linear problem in the Miles–Howard regime
\beta^{2}>\tfrac{1}{4}
and to confirm Hartman’s conjectured growth of the vorticity. The method was later refined to treat the full nonlinear equations: in
[
BBCZD21
]
, the authors proved the asymptotic stability of (
1.6
) over a finite but long time interval, during which the velocity field and density exhibit constant inviscid damping while the vorticity grows in accordance with Hartman’s predictions. See also the recent review
[
bianchini2022symmetrization
]
.

The techniques developed in all the above results rely on the unboundedness of the vertical variable
y\in{\mathbb{R}}
and on the Fourier-analytic tools available in that setting. As such, the case of the periodic channel
{\mathbb{T}}\times[0,2]
requires a completely different approach. In
[
CZN25chan
]
, the authors studied the linear asymptotic stability of the Couette flow (
1.6
) with
\beta^{2}>0
by means of the
limiting absorption principle
, which consists in capturing refined resolvent estimates near the spectrum of the linearized operator.

In the periodic channel, the spectrum of the linearized operator
{\mathcal{L}}
exhibits several features absent in the periodic strip. For
\beta^{2}>\tfrac{1}{4}
,
[
CZN25chan
]
proves the existence of two infinite sequences of neutral eigenvalues converging to the endpoints of the essential spectrum of
{\mathcal{L}}
. For
\beta^{2}=\tfrac{1}{4}
, the spectrum of
{\mathcal{L}}
is purely continuous, with no discrete (stable, unstable, or neutral) eigenvalues. For
\beta^{2}<\tfrac{1}{4}
, there are no neutral eigenvalues, but the Miles–Howard criterion no longer applies, and unstable discrete eigenvalues may appear. Note that this spectral structure is not favorable for the nonlinear problem, since the presence of neutral or unstable eigenvalues would complicate the analysis. Indeed, linearly unstable eigenvalues may lead to nonlinear instabilities, while neutral eigenvalues, though they may not cause growth, might still prevent decay of the velocity field.

1.2.
Main results

The goal of this article is to understand in depth the linear asymptotic stability of stratified monotone shear flows, beyond the Couette flow with a linear density stratification.

The spectral properties of the linearised Boussinesq operator
{\mathcal{L}}
at a general shear-type stationary solution need not, in general, be favourable for the stability of the shear flow. Indeed, if
{\mathcal{J}}(y)<\frac{1}{4}
in a non-empty subset of
[0,2]
, the Miles–Howard stability criterion no longer applies, so it is not clear whether unstable eigenvalues exist or not. Likewise, if there are points where
{\mathcal{J}}(y)>\frac{1}{4}
the existence of neutral eigenvalues is not ruled out either.

Therefore, we will need several natural assumptions on the equilibrium solution (
1.2
) that we want to perturb. We will formulate these hypotheses in terms of the background shear flow
v(y)
and the functions

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}(y):=-\partial_{y}\overline{\rho},\qquad{\mathcal{P}}(y):={\mathfrak{g}}\mathrm{P}(y),\qquad{\mathcal{J}}(y):=\frac{{\mathcal{P}}(y)}{v^{\prime}(y)^{2}}.

Physically,
\mathrm{P}
describes the density stratification and
{\mathcal{J}}
is the local Richardson number.

Specifically, we will need two sets of hypotheses. Firstly, we assume that

•

H
{\mathcal{P}}
:
{\mathcal{P}}(y)\in C^{2}([0,2])
with
{\mathcal{P}}(y)=0
for all
y\in[0,\vartheta_{1}]\cup[\vartheta_{2},2]
and
{\mathcal{P}}(y)>0
for all
y\in(\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2})
, for some
0<\vartheta_{1}<\vartheta_{2}<1
.

•

H
v
:
v(y)\in C^{4}([0,2])
with
0<c_{0}\leq v^{\prime}(y)\leq C_{0}
for some
0<c_{0}<C_{0}
and
\text{supp }v^{\prime\prime}\subseteq(\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2})
.

Second, we also impose the following:

H1

There holds
c_{0}^{-1}\|v^{\prime\prime\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,2)}+\frac{1}{2}c_{0}^{-2}\|{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,2)}<1
.

H2

There exists a unique
\tilde{y}\in[0,2]
such that
{{\mathcal{J}}}(\tilde{y})=\max_{y\in[0,2]}{{\mathcal{J}}}(y)
. Moreover,

H2.1

On
[0,\tilde{y}]
,
v^{\prime\prime}(y)\leq 0
and
{\mathcal{J}}^{\prime}(y)\geq 0
.

H2.2

On
[\tilde{y},2]
,
v^{\prime\prime}(y)\geq 0
and
{\mathcal{J}}^{\prime}(y)\leq 0
.

H2.3

The equation
{\mathcal{J}}(y)=\frac{1}{4}
has exactly two distinct solutions in
[0,2]
.

H3

The linearised Euler operator
{\mathcal{L}}_{E}\omega:=v(y)\partial_{x}\omega-v^{\prime\prime}(y){\Delta}^{-1}\partial_{x}\omega
has no eigenvalue nor embedded eigenvalue.

Remark 1.1
.

The condition
c_{0}^{-1}\|v^{\prime\prime\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,2)}<1
suffices to show that
{\mathcal{L}}_{E}
has no embedded eigenvalues, but it does not rule out the existence of discrete eigenvalues outside the continuous spectrum. Thus,
H3
is not superfluous next to
H1
.
Assumption H2.3 ensures that there are two distinct regions where
{\mathcal{J}}\approx\frac{1}{4}
and exactly two roots of the equation
{\mathcal{J}}(y)=\frac{1}{4}
. This is a simplification that we performed to keep the focus on the strong and weak regions. Indeed, if
{\mathcal{J}}(y)=\frac{1}{4}
on an interval we can use the ideas presented in
[
CZN25chan
]
to show that Theorems
B
and
D
below remain true there: the Green’s function in such a region is still well-defined with analogous estimates to those presented in Section
5
and the homogeneous solutions constructed for the spectral theory in Section
7.2
are defined so that they already contain the appropriate logarithmic corrections.

The starting point of our analysis is the observation that, under these hypotheses, the linearised operator has purely continuous spectrum:

Theorem A
.

Assume that hypotheses H
{\mathcal{P}}
, H
v
and H1–H3 hold. Then, the spectrum of
{\mathcal{L}}
is purely continuous; there are no eigenvalues or embedded eigenvalues.

We emphasize that Theorem
A
is of independent interest, apart from the stability estimates that we shall state next. Indeed, characterising the spectrum of the linearised Boussinesq equations near stably stratified monotone shear flows has attracted attention in the physics community for quite some time, and despite several partial results (see
[
Hirota
,
Miles
,
Howard
]
and
[
Yaglom
, Section 3.2.2]
), no unifying description of the full spectrum had been available. Under assumptions
H1
–
H3
, Theorem
A
provides the first rigorous and complete characterization of the spectral properties of the linearized operator. On the other hand, while
H1
–
H3
are sufficient to ensure the absence of discrete or embedded eigenvalues of
{\mathcal{L}}
, in Sections
8
and
10
we shall provide further insights on the main structural conditions required for Theorem
B
to hold.

Once we have made sure that the spectrum of
{\mathcal{L}}
might lead to the spectral dispersive effects that underlie inviscid damping, we turn to the analysis of the asymptotic stability of the linearised equations. For this, since it is already known from the analysis of the Couette flow
[
BCZD22
,
YL18
,
CZN25chan
,
CZN23strip
]
that the Richardson number controls the dynamics of the linearised equations, it is necessary to tag the positions where there is a qualitative change in the behavior of the local Richardson number.
Consequently, let us divide the interval
[0,2]
according to the behavior of
{\mathcal{J}}(y)
. For this, we fix some
0<\tilde{\delta}<\frac{1}{8}
and note that, as a consequence of hypothesis H2, there exists
\varpi_{1},\varpi_{2}\in(0,2)
and
\varpi_{n,j}\in(0,2)
, for
n,j=1,2
such that:

(1)

\vartheta_{1}<\varpi_{1,1}<\varpi_{1}<\varpi_{1,2}<\varpi_{2,1}<\varpi_{2}<\varpi_{2,2}<\vartheta_{2}
.

(2)

{\mathcal{J}}(\varpi_{1})={\mathcal{J}}(\varpi_{2})=\frac{1}{4}
.

(3)

{\mathcal{J}}(y)>\frac{1}{4}
for all
y\in(\varpi_{1},\varpi_{2})
.

(4)

0<{\mathcal{J}}(y)<\frac{1}{4}
for all
y\in(\vartheta_{1},\varpi_{1})\cup(\varpi_{2},\vartheta_{2})
.

(5)

|{\mathcal{J}}(y)-\frac{1}{4}|\leq\tilde{\delta}
, for all
y\in(\varpi_{1,1},\varpi_{1,2})\cup(\varpi_{2,1},\varpi_{2,2})
.

We will refer to the regions defined by these positions as
non-stratified
(
[0,\vartheta_{1}]\cup[\vartheta_{2},2]
),
weakly stratified
(
(\vartheta_{1},\varpi_{1,1})\cup(\varpi_{2,2},\vartheta_{2})
),
mildly stratified
(
[\varpi_{1,1},\varpi_{1,2}]\cup[\varpi_{2,1},\varpi_{2,2}]
) and
strongly stratified
(
(\varpi_{1,2},\varpi_{2,1})
).

Using this notation, we are ready to present our main result , which is the linear inviscid damping of the perturbed velocity and density, with time-decay rates that depend on the local Richardson number
{\mathcal{J}}(y)
. To this end, we define

\displaystyle\mu(y):={\rm Re}\sqrt{\frac{1}{4}-{\mathcal{J}}(y)}.

Theorem B
.

Assume that the initial data
({\omega}^{0},\rho^{0})
is compactly supported inside
(\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2})
and that hypotheses H
{\mathcal{P}}
, H
v
and H1–H3 hold. Writing
\rho^{0}(y)=\mathrm{P}(y)\varrho^{0}(y)
, we also assume that

\int_{\mathbb{T}}{\omega}^{0}(x,y)\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\varrho^{0}(x,y)\mathrm{d}x=0.

(1.7)

Let
\boldsymbol{v}=(v^{x},v^{y})=\nabla^{\perp}\psi=(-\partial_{y}\psi,\partial_{x}\psi)
be the corresponding velocity field. There exists a constant
C>0
such that, for all
t\geq 1
, the solution decays as follows:

•

Non-stratified region: for
y\in[0,\vartheta_{1}]\cup[\vartheta_{2},2]
,

\displaystyle\|v^{x}(t,x,y)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{-1}\left(\|\omega^{0}\|_{H^{1/2}_{x}H^{2}_{y}}+\|\varrho^{0}\|_{H^{1/2}_{x}H^{3}_{y}}\right),

(1.8)

\displaystyle\|v^{y}(t,x,y)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{-2}\left(\|\omega^{0}\|_{H^{1/2}_{x}H^{3}_{y}}+\|\varrho^{0}\|_{H^{1/2}_{x}H^{4}_{y}}\right),

(1.9)

\displaystyle\|\rho(t,x,y)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle=0.

(1.10)

•

Weakly stratified region: for
y\in(\vartheta_{1},\varpi_{1,1})\cup(\varpi_{2,2},\vartheta_{2})
,

\displaystyle\|v^{x}(t,x,y)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mu(y)}\left(\|\omega^{0}\|_{H^{1/2}_{x}H^{2}_{y}}+\|\varrho^{0}\|_{H^{1/2}_{x}H^{3}_{y}}\right),

(1.11)

\displaystyle\|v^{y}(t,x,y)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{-\frac{3}{2}+\mu(y)}\left(\|\omega^{0}\|_{H^{1/2}_{x}H^{3}_{y}}+\|\varrho^{0}\|_{H^{1/2}_{x}H^{4}_{y}}\right),

(1.12)

\displaystyle\|\rho(t,x,y)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mu(y)}\left(\|\omega^{0}\|_{H^{1/2}_{x}H^{2}_{y}}+\|\varrho^{0}\|_{H^{1/2}_{x}H^{3}_{y}}\right).

(1.13)

•

Mildly stratified region: for
y\in[\varpi_{1,1},\varpi_{1,2}]\cup[\varpi_{2,1},\varpi_{2,2}]
,

\displaystyle\|v^{x}(t,x,y)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mu(y)}(1+\log t)\left(\|\omega^{0}\|_{H^{1/2}_{x}H^{2}_{y}}+\|\varrho^{0}\|_{H^{1/2}_{x}H^{3}_{y}}\right),

(1.14)

\displaystyle\|v^{y}(t,x,y)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{-\frac{3}{2}+\mu(y)}(1+\log t)\left(\|\omega^{0}\|_{H^{1/2}_{x}H^{3}_{y}}+\|\varrho^{0}\|_{H^{1/2}_{x}H^{4}_{y}}\right),

(1.15)

\displaystyle\|\rho(t,x,y)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mu(y)}(1+\log t)\left(\|\omega^{0}\|_{H^{1/2}_{x}H^{2}_{y}}+\|\varrho^{0}\|_{H^{1/2}_{x}H^{3}_{y}}\right).

(1.16)

•

Strongly stratified region: for
y\in(\varpi_{1,2},\varpi_{2,1})
,

\displaystyle\|v^{x}(t,x,y)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left(\|\omega^{0}\|_{H^{1/2}_{x}H^{2}_{y}}+\|\varrho^{0}\|_{H^{1/2}_{x}H^{3}_{y}}\right),

(1.17)

\displaystyle\|v^{y}(t,x,y)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{-\frac{3}{2}}\left(\|\omega^{0}\|_{H^{1/2}_{x}H^{3}_{y}}+\|\varrho^{0}\|_{H^{1/2}_{x}H^{4}_{y}}\right),

(1.18)

\displaystyle\|\rho(t,x,y)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left(\|\omega^{0}\|_{H^{1/2}_{x}H^{2}_{y}}+\|\varrho^{0}\|_{H^{1/2}_{x}H^{3}_{y}}\right).

(1.19)

Remark 1.2
.

It is natural to wonder where the obtained time-decay rates come from, and whether they are optimal. In Subsection
1.4
below, we have included a relatively short heuristic argument that, in a non-rigorous way, suggests that these should be the optimal exponents. It is therefore reasonable to expect that the decay estimates of Theorem
B
(and the derivative bounds of Theorem
D
below) are likely sharp. Perhaps this could be easily seen in the case of linearised equations on the periodic strip which admit explicit solutions
[
CZN23strip
]
; in fact, the quantity
\|\omega\|_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla\rho\|_{L^{2}}
is known to grow like
t^{\frac{1}{2}}
for the full non-linear problem
[
BBCZD21
]
.

Several comments are now in order. First, note that the zero average condition (
1.7
) is standard in all linear inviscid damping results, since the averages of the initial data are otherwise preserved by the linear evolution of the equations. The inviscid damping decay rates (
1.8
)–(
1.19
) are pointwise in
y\in[0,2]
and describe the asymptotic stability of the stably stratified shear flow
(\overline{\boldsymbol{v}},\overline{\rho})
. The velocity field and density at
y\in[0,2]
decay in time with rates that crucially depend on the local Richardson number
{\mathcal{J}}(y)
through
\mu(y)
. In the strongly stratified regime
\mu(y)=0
and the decay rates are faster than in the weakly stratified regime, where
\mu(y)>0
. The transition region between the strongly stratified region and weakly stratified region experiences a logarithmic loss. Because of the compact support assumptions, in the non-stratified region the equations locally reduce to the Euler equations of motion, and the usual inviscid-damping decay rates for the velocity field are recovered. Likewise, the compact support of the density perturbation is preserved: due to the support assumptions on
{\mathcal{P}}(y)
and
\rho^{0}(y)
, no density is transported into the non-stratified regime.

Second, note that, as is standard in inviscid damping estimates, to quantify the decay of the solutions we need bounds on fairly high Sobolev norms of the initial datum. This is because a key underlying mechanism behind the decay estimates is the trade off of regularity of the initial data for decay in time, so that sufficiently smooth initial data is needed to reach the desired decay rates. For instance, the inviscid damping for
\|v^{y}(t,x,y)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}
needs
\omega^{0}\in H^{1/2}_{x}H^{3}_{y}
and
\varrho^{0}\in H^{1/2}_{x}H^{4}_{y}
. These effects, which will also be apparent in other ensuing theorems, will be discussed in Section
14
.

Third, let us say a few words about the support condition, which is crucially used in the proofs. Motivated by the non-linear inviscid damping results for the Euler equations in the periodic channel, our goal is to ensure that the linear evolution of the vorticity and density remains compactly supported away from the physical boundaries. Unlike the linearised Euler equations, we observe from (
1.1
) that having
v^{\prime\prime}(y)
and
\omega^{0}
compactly supported is not sufficient to ensure that
\omega
remains compactly supported inside the periodic channel. Indeed, the vorticity perturbation is now transported by
v(y)\partial_{x}
but also forced by
-{\mathfrak{g}}\partial_{x}\rho
. In turn, the density
\rho
is transported by
v(y)\partial_{x}
and crucially forced by
-\partial_{y}\overline{\rho}\partial_{x}\psi
, which is a non-local operator and in general it will not be zero near the boundary. Therefore, for
\rho
not to be forced near the boundary we need
-\partial_{y}\overline{\rho}
to be compactly supported in the channel. This is the main reason behind the choice of our density profile as in H
{\mathcal{P}}
. Then,
\rho(t)
is compactly supported in the channel if
\rho^{0}
itself is so. Consequently,
\omega(t)
is also compactly supported once
\omega^{0}
is so as well.

Once
{\mathcal{P}}(y)
is supported in
[\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2}]
, it is natural to assume that the perturbed density is in fact compactly supported inside
(\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2})
, since otherwise it would be the main leading term responsible for the buoyancy forces outside
[\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2}]
and the dynamics would be much more complicated. This rather heuristic justification becomes transparent in Section
2
, where
\rho^{0}
is used to regularize the equation governing the evolution of the velocity field.

Since our main goal in this paper is to understand the effects of stratification, we likewise assume that
\text{supp }\omega^{0}\subset(\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2})
, which is a simplification of the more essential condition
\text{supp }\omega^{0}\subset(0,2)
. While the former ensures that the vorticity remains supported in the stratified region, which is enough for our purposes, the later allows vorticity in the non-stratified region, and away from the physical boundaries. As we shall see in Section
11
, the dynamics of the linearised equations outside
(\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2})
are closely related to those of the linearised Euler equations nearby a monotone shear flow: the relevant inviscid damping (
1.8
)-(
1.9
) and vorticity scattering can be obtained for initial vorticity supported outside the stratified region following the ideas of
[
Jia20
,
JiaGev20
]
.

As a minor remark, we mention that assumptions
H1
and
H2.3
cannot hold simultaneously if they are posed in the shorter yet more usual interval
[0,1]
, the main reason being that
{\mathcal{P}}
does not grow sufficiently fast in
[0,1]
so that
{\mathcal{J}}(y)<\frac{1}{4}
for all
y\in[0,1]
. Hence, there are no strongly nor mildly stratified regions, and the situation is less rich. Of course, this is not a limitation of the method of proof: the analysis remains valid and the statements are essentially the same. In particular, Theorem
A
remains true on
[0,1]
and the estimates of Theorems
B
-
E
now apply in the non-stratified region
[0,\vartheta_{1}]\cup[\vartheta_{1},1]
and the weakly stratified region
(\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2})
. The size of the channel and a weak Richardson number
{\mathcal{J}}<\frac{1}{4}
influence the stability properties of a family of background steady states (
1.2
), which has been recently addressed in
[
bianchini2025ill
]
.

Therefore, for concreteness, we have opted to work in the broader channel
{\mathbb{T}}\times[0,2]
, which admits steady states (
1.2
) for which
H1
–
H3
holds and the strongly stratified region is non-empty. Such an example would be the Couette flow
v_{C}(y)=y
paired with a suitable compactly supported stratification
{\mathcal{P}}(y)\in C^{2}
such that
\|{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,1)}\leq 2
, non-decreasing in
[0,1]
, symmetric with respect to
y=1
and with
{\mathcal{P}}(1)>\frac{1}{4}
. We conclude by noting that increasing the size of the channel from
[0,2]
to
[0,\mathsf{H}]
for some
\mathsf{H}>2
is automatic: this enlarges the family of steady states (
1.2
) for which
H1
–
H3
holds, the strongly stratified region (
{\mathcal{J}}>\frac{1}{4}
) increases, and the conclusions of Theorems
A
-
E
remain true.

1.3.
Refinements and further estimates

Here we present a few additional results that complement our main inviscid damping estimate, Theorem
B
.

One should note that the decay rates (
1.8
)–(
1.10
) are not the continuous limit as
y\rightarrow\vartheta_{1}
or
y\rightarrow\vartheta_{2}
of the weaker rates (
1.11
)-(
1.13
). Indeed, those rates degenerate into merely boundedness of
\boldsymbol{v}^{x}
and
\rho
and to
t^{-1}
decay of
v^{y}
, thus representing a loss of a full
t^{-1}
power. While this mismatch is difficult to reconcile, we are nevertheless able to locally sharpen (
1.11
)–(
1.13
). This constitutes our second main result, which we state next. Estimates analogous to (
1.20
)-(
1.22
) also hold for
y\in[0,\varpi_{1,1})
, with
(y-\vartheta_{1})_{+}
replacing
(\vartheta_{2}-y)_{+}
.

Theorem C
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
B
, for all
t\geq 1
and
y\in(\varpi_{2,2},2]
, there holds

\displaystyle\|v^{x}(t,x,y)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq C\min\left(t^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mu(y)},t^{-1}+(\vartheta_{2}-y)_{+}\right)\left(\|\omega^{0}\|_{H^{3/2}_{x}H^{3}_{y}}+\|\varrho^{0}\|_{H^{3/2}_{x}H^{4}_{y}}\right),

(1.20)

\displaystyle\|v^{y}(t,x,y)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq C\min\left(t^{-\frac{3}{2}+\mu(y)},t^{-2}+(\vartheta_{2}-y)_{+}t^{-1}\right)\left(\|\omega^{0}\|_{H^{3/2}_{x}H^{3}_{y}}+\|\varrho^{0}\|_{H^{3/2}_{x}H^{4}_{y}}\right),

(1.21)

\displaystyle\|\rho(t,x,y)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq C\min\left(t^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mu(y)},\mathrm{P}(y)(1+\log t)\right)\left(\|\omega^{0}\|_{H^{1/2}_{x}H^{2}_{y}}+\|\varrho^{0}\|_{H^{1/2}_{x}H^{3}_{y}}\right).

(1.22)

Note that Theorem
C
shows that, for all
t\geq 1
, the decay estimates (though not the rates) (
1.20
)-(
1.22
) are now continuous in
y\in[0,\varpi_{1,1})\cup(\varpi_{2,2},2]
. Namely, the velocity field can be decomposed into an Euler-type fast-decaying part, together with a slower contribution that vanishes in the non-stratified region.

In addition to the bounds for the velocity and density presented so far, it is convenient to consider the evolution of the vorticity and of the density gradient.
This is because, in the Boussinesq equations (
1.1
), the vorticity is not only transported by the velocity field, but also forced by
\partial_{x}\rho
. Thus, there is no reason for the
L^{\infty}
norm of the vorticity to be preserved. In fact, for bounded in time forcing, the vorticity could grow up to linearly in time. The density perturbation is also transported by the velocity field
v(y)\partial_{x}
and forced by
\mathrm{P}(y)v^{y}(t,x,y)
. Since
v(y)
is smooth and strictly monotone,
\partial_{y}\rho
should experience at most linear growth as the fluid is stretched by the mixing effects.

Our next result asserts that such at most linear-in-time growth is damped by the stabilizing mechanism responsible for the decay of
v(t,x,y)
and
\rho(t,x,y)
, thereby justifying the (upper bound in the) predictions made by Hartman
[
Hartman
]
in the periodic channel.

Theorem D
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
B
, there exists
C>0
such that, for all
t\geq 1
,

•

Non-stratified region: for
y\in[0,\vartheta_{1}]\cup[\vartheta_{2},2]
,

\displaystyle\|\omega(t,x,y)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle=0,

(1.23)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{y}\rho(t,x,y)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle=0.

(1.24)

•

Weakly stratified region: for
y\in(\vartheta_{1},\varpi_{1,1})\cup(\varpi_{2,2},\vartheta_{2})
,

\displaystyle\|\omega(t,x,y)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{\frac{1}{2}+\mu(y)}\left(\|\omega^{0}\|_{H^{3/2}_{x}H^{3}_{y}}+\|\varrho^{0}\|_{H^{3/2}_{x}H^{4}_{y}}\right),

(1.25)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{y}\rho(t,x,y)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{\frac{1}{2}+\mu(y)}\left(\|\omega^{0}\|_{H^{3/2}_{x}H^{3}_{y}}+\|\varrho^{0}\|_{H^{3/2}_{x}H^{4}_{y}}\right).

(1.26)

•

Mildly stratified region: for
y\in[\varpi_{1,1},\varpi_{1,2}]\cup[\varpi_{2,1},\varpi_{2,2}]
,

\displaystyle\|\omega(t,x,y)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{\frac{1}{2}+\mu(y)}(1+\log t)\left(\|\omega^{0}\|_{H^{3/2}_{x}H^{3}_{y}}+\|\varrho^{0}\|_{H^{3/2}_{x}H^{4}_{y}}\right),

(1.27)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{y}\rho(t,x,y)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{\frac{1}{2}+\mu(y)}(1+\log t)\left(\|\omega^{0}\|_{H^{3/2}_{x}H^{3}_{y}}+\|\varrho^{0}\|_{H^{3/2}_{x}H^{4}_{y}}\right).

(1.28)

•

Strongly stratified region: for
y\in(\varpi_{1,2},\varpi_{2,1})
,

\displaystyle\|\omega(t,x,y)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\|\omega^{0}\|_{H^{3/2}_{x}H^{3}_{y}}+\|\varrho^{0}\|_{H^{3/2}_{x}H^{4}_{y}}\right),

(1.29)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{y}\rho(t,x,y)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\|\omega^{0}\|_{H^{3/2}_{x}H^{3}_{y}}+\|\varrho^{0}\|_{H^{3/2}_{x}H^{4}_{y}}\right).

(1.30)

The compact support of the initial data, of
v^{\prime\prime}(y)
, and of
{\mathcal{P}}(y)
ensures that the vorticity remains compactly supported within the stratified region. The growth bounds (
1.29
)–(
1.30
) are sublinear in time due to the inviscid damping experienced by
v(t,x,y)
and
\rho(t,x,y)
. While in the strongly stratified regime the growth rate is at most
t^{\frac{1}{2}}
, we observe that in the weakly stratified region the growth rate becomes linear as
y\to\vartheta_{1}
or
y\to\vartheta_{2}
, since the stabilizing effects of inviscid damping weaken and ultimately vanish there. However, we can refine these bounds in the spirit of Theorem
C
, so that they converge to those of the non-stratified setting:

Theorem E
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
B
, for
y\in(\varpi_{2,2},2]
and all
t\geq 1
, there holds

\displaystyle\|\omega(t,x,y)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq C\min\left(t^{\frac{1}{2}+\mu(y)},1+(\vartheta_{2}-y)_{+}t\right)\left(\|\omega^{0}\|_{H^{5/2}_{x}H^{3}_{y}}+\|\varrho^{0}\|_{H^{5/2}_{x}H^{4}_{y}}\right),

(1.31)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{y}\rho(t,x,y)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\mathrm{P}^{\prime}(y)(1+\log t)+t\min\left(t^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mu(y)},\mathrm{P}(y)(1+\log t)\right)\right)

(1.32)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\left(\|\omega^{0}\|_{H^{3/2}_{x}H^{3}_{y}}+\|\varrho^{0}\|_{H^{3/2}_{x}H^{4}_{y}}\right).

In particular, for any fixed time
t\geq 1
, the estimates (
1.31
) and (
1.32
) capture the limiting behaviour as
y\to\vartheta_{2}
expected from (
1.23
) and (
1.24
), while also retaining the sublinear growth described by (
1.25
) and (
1.26
) in the long-time limit.

1.4.
A heuristic derivation of the decay exponents

As anticipated above, we now present a non-rigorous derivation of the inviscid damping decay rates (
1.8
)-(
1.19
). Formally, the solution
(\omega(t),\rho(t))
to the linearised Boussinesq equations (
1.4
) can be written as

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\omega\\
\rho\end{pmatrix}(t)=e^{-t{\mathcal{L}}}\begin{pmatrix}\omega^{0}\\
\rho^{0}\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\partial\Omega}e^{-\lambda t}(\lambda-{\mathcal{L}})^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\omega^{0}\\
\rho^{0}\end{pmatrix}\mathrm{d}\lambda

where
\Omega
denotes an open set in the complex plane
{\mathbb{C}}
containing the spectrum of
{\mathcal{L}}
. Since
{\mathcal{L}}
decouples in the
x
-variable, for

\displaystyle\omega(t,x,y)=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\omega_{k}(t,y)e^{ikx},\quad\rho(t,x,y)=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\rho(t,y)e^{ikx}

the evolution for each
k
mode is given by

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\omega_{k}\\
\rho_{k}\end{pmatrix}(t,y)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\partial\Omega}e^{-\lambda t}(\lambda-ik{\mathcal{L}}_{k})^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\omega_{k}^{0}\\
\rho_{k}^{0}\end{pmatrix}\mathrm{d}\lambda,\quad{\mathcal{L}}_{k}=\begin{pmatrix}v(y)-v^{\prime\prime}(y)\Delta_{k}^{-1}&{\mathfrak{g}}\\
-\mathrm{P}(y)\Delta_{k}^{-1}&v(y)\end{pmatrix}

with
\Delta_{k}=\partial_{y}^{2}-k^{2}
. If the spectrum of
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}
consists only of a purely continuous part that coincides with the range of
v(y)
, then for
\lambda=ikv(y_{0})
with
y_{0}\in[0,2]
we may write

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\omega_{k}\\
\rho_{k}\end{pmatrix}(t,y)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{0}^{2}e^{-ikv(y_{0})t}(v(y_{0})-{\mathcal{L}}_{k})^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\omega_{k}^{0}\\
\rho_{k}^{0}\end{pmatrix}v^{\prime}(y_{0})\mathrm{d}y_{0}.

Since we are interested in the long-time dynamics of the velocity field, for
\psi_{k}(t,y)=\Delta_{k}^{-1}\omega_{k}(t,y)
we now observe that

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{k}\\
\rho_{k}\end{pmatrix}(t,y)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{0}^{2}e^{-ikv(y_{0})t}\begin{pmatrix}\varphi_{k}(y,y_{0})\\
\rho_{k}(y,y_{0})\end{pmatrix}v^{\prime}(y_{0})\mathrm{d}y_{0},

(1.33)

where the pair
(\Delta_{k}\varphi_{k},\rho_{k})
is the unique solution to the resolvent equations

\displaystyle(v(y_{0})-{\mathcal{L}}_{k})\begin{pmatrix}\Delta_{k}\varphi_{k}\\
\rho_{k}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\omega^{0}\\
\rho^{0}\end{pmatrix},

which are equivalent to

\displaystyle\Delta_{k}\varphi_{k}(y,y_{0})-\frac{v^{\prime\prime}(y)}{v(y)-v(y_{0})}\varphi_{k}(y,y_{0})+\frac{{\mathcal{P}}(y)}{(v(y)-v(y_{0}))^{2}}\varphi_{k}(y,y_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{\omega_{k}^{0}(y)}{v(y)-v(y_{0})}-\frac{{\mathfrak{g}}\rho_{k}^{0}(y)}{(v(y)-v(y_{0})^{2}},

(1.34)

\displaystyle\rho_{k}(y,y_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{\rho_{k}^{0}+\mathrm{P}(y)\varphi_{k}(y,y_{0})}{v(y)-v(y_{0})}

(1.35)

together with
\varphi_{k}(0,y_{0})=\varphi_{k}(2,y_{0})=0
. Already from (
1.33
) we observe that time decay for
(\psi_{k},\rho_{k})
may be obtained integrating by parts, if the resolvent functions
(\varphi_{k},\rho_{k})
are sufficiently smooth. However, we also already see that equations (
1.34
) and (
1.35
) governing
(\varphi_{k},\rho_{k})
exhibit a potential singularity for
y
near
y_{0}
and thus the regularity of the resolvent functions is not a priori straightforward.

Nevertheless, for all
y_{0}\in(\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2})
we recall that
{\mathcal{P}}(y_{0})>0
and, heuristically, homogeneous solution
\phi_{k}(y,y_{0})
to (
1.34
) should locally satisfy

\displaystyle\partial_{y}^{2}\phi_{k}(y,y_{0})-k^{2}\phi_{k}(y,y_{0})+\frac{{\mathcal{J}}(y_{0})}{(y-y_{0})^{2}}\phi_{k}(y,y_{0})=0.

(1.36)

since
{\mathcal{J}}(y_{0})=\frac{{\mathcal{P}}(y_{0})}{(v^{\prime}(y_{0}))^{2}}
. Inspired by the Euler index formula for ordinary differential equations, we ansatz
\phi_{k}(y,y_{0})=(2k(y-y_{0}))^{\alpha}\mathcal{E}_{k}(y,y_{0})
, for some
\alpha>0
and some smooth function
\mathcal{E}_{k}
with
\mathcal{E}_{k}(y_{0},y_{0})=1
. Then, plugging the ansatz into (
1.36
), multiplying by
(2k(y-y_{0}))^{2-\alpha}
and evaluating at
y=y_{0}
we obtain

\displaystyle 4k^{2}\alpha(\alpha-1)-4k^{2}{\mathcal{J}}(y_{0})=0,

whose two solutions are

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}(y_{0})=\frac{1}{2}+\gamma(y_{0}),\quad\alpha_{2}(y_{0})=\frac{1}{2}-\gamma(y_{0}),\quad\gamma(y_{0})=\sqrt{\frac{1}{4}-{\mathcal{J}}(y_{0})}.

Consequently, the most singular homogeneous solution
\psi_{{\mathsf{s}},k}
should behave like

\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k}(y,y_{0})\approx(2k(y-y_{0}))^{\frac{1}{2}-\gamma(y_{0})},

so that the regularity of solutions non-trivially depend on
{\mathcal{J}}(y_{0})
, the local Richardson number. It is more regular for
y_{0}\in(\varpi_{1,2},\varpi_{2,1})
the strongly stratified regime since there
{\mathcal{J}}(y_{0})>\frac{1}{4}
and thus
\gamma(y_{0})=i\nu(y_{0})
, while in the weakly stratified regime
\mu(y_{0})\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
for
y_{0}\in(\vartheta_{1},\varpi_{1,1})\cup(\varpi_{2,2},\vartheta_{2})
, with
\mu(y)\rightarrow\frac{1}{2}
for
y_{0}\rightarrow\vartheta_{1}
or
y_{0}\rightarrow\vartheta_{2}
, which corresponds to worse regularity for
\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k}
. We further remark here that for
y_{0}\in(0,2)
with
{\mathcal{J}}(y_{0})=\frac{1}{4}
two roots
\alpha_{1}(y_{0})=\alpha_{2}(y_{0})=\frac{1}{2}
, the associated homogeneous solutions are then not linearly independent and a logarithmic correction needs to be introduced.

Coming back to (
1.33
) and further supposing that the resolvent solutions locally behave like the homogeneous solutions to (
1.34
), we have that

\displaystyle\psi_{k}(t,y)

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{0}^{2}e^{-ikv(y_{0})t}(2k(y-y_{0}))^{\frac{1}{2}-\mu(y_{0})}v^{\prime}(y_{0})\mathrm{d}y_{0}

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{0}^{2}e^{-ikv(y_{0})t}(2k(y-y_{0}))^{\frac{1}{2}-\mu(y)}v^{\prime}(y_{0})\mathrm{d}y_{0}

because
\text{exp}\left(\left|\mu(y)-\mu(y_{0})\right|\left|\log(|2k(y-y_{0})|)\right|\right)\lesssim 1
whenever
2k|y-y_{0}|\lesssim 1
. Disregarding any boundary contributions, integrating by parts once,

\displaystyle\psi_{k}(t,y)

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{k(1-2\mu(y))}{ikt}\int_{0}^{2}e^{-ikv(y_{0})t}(2k(y-y_{0}))^{-\frac{1}{2}-\mu(y)}\mathrm{d}y_{0}

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{k(1-2\mu(y))}{ikt}\int_{y-\delta}^{y+\delta}e^{-ikv(y_{0})t}(2k(y-y_{0}))^{-\frac{1}{2}-\mu(y)}\mathrm{d}y_{0}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{k(1-2\mu(y))}{ikt}\int_{(0,2)\setminus(y-\delta,y+\delta)}e^{-ikv(y_{0})t}(2k(y-y_{0}))^{-\frac{1}{2}-\mu(y)}\mathrm{d}y_{0}

for some
\delta>0
. Integrating back, we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{k(1-2\mu(y))}{ikt}\int_{y-\delta}^{y+\delta}e^{-ikv(y_{0})t}(2k(y-y_{0}))^{-\frac{1}{2}-\mu(y)}\mathrm{d}y_{0}\right|\lesssim k^{-\frac{1}{2}}(k\delta)^{\frac{1}{2}-\mu(y)}

while integrating by parts once more, discarding any possible boundary contributions, taking the modulus and then integrating back also gives

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{k(1-2\mu(y))}{ikt}\int_{(0,2)\setminus(y-\delta,y+\delta)}e^{-ikv(y_{0})t}(2k(y-y_{0}))^{-\frac{1}{2}-\mu(y)}\mathrm{d}y_{0}\right|

\displaystyle\qquad=\left|\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{k^{2}{\mathcal{J}}(y)}{(ikt)^{2}}\int_{(0,2)\setminus(y-\delta,y+\delta)}e^{-ikv(y_{0})t}(v^{\prime}(y_{0}))^{-1}(2k(y-y_{0}))^{-\frac{3}{2}-\mu(y)}\mathrm{d}y_{0}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim k^{-\frac{1}{2}}t^{-2}(k\delta)^{-\frac{1}{2}-\mu(y)}

Optimizing in
\delta>0
, we see that
k\delta=\frac{1}{t}
gives the best scaling and produces
|\psi_{k}(t,y)|\lesssim k^{-\frac{1}{2}}t^{-\frac{3}{2}+\mu(y)}
, which is the claimed decay rate. The estimates for
v^{x}_{k}(t,y)=\partial_{y}\psi_{k}(t,y)
and
\rho_{k}(t,y)
follow similarly. Let us finish by observing that for
y_{0}\in[0,\vartheta_{1}]\cup[\vartheta_{2},2]
we have
v^{\prime\prime}(y_{0})={\mathcal{P}}(y_{0})=0
, so that (
1.34
) becomes the Rayleigh equation for the Couette shear flow, whose solutions enjoy better regularity. This is the main reason why the inviscid damping decay rates (
1.8
) - (
1.10
) for
y\in[0,\vartheta_{1}]\cup[\vartheta_{2},2]
are those obtained for the Euler equations near the Couette flow.

1.5.
Literature review

In the absence of gravity and for constant density, the Boussinesq equations (
1.1
) reduce to the well-known two-dimensional Euler equations, for which shear flows
\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}=(v(y),0)
are steady-state solutions. The asymptotic stability of such shear flows in ideal fluids has been investigated since the work of Lord Kelvin
[
kelvin1887stability
]
. Later, Orr
[
orr1907stability
]
discovered that the linear transport of vorticity induced by the background shear produces a mixing mechanism, leading to weak convergence of the perturbed vorticity toward its average and to decay in time of the associated velocity field.

For for the full non-linear Euler equations, these effects were first captured near the Couette flow in the periodic strip
{\mathbb{T}}\times{\mathbb{R}}
in the ground-breaking work of Bedrossian and Masmoudi
[
BM15
]
. Since then, there has been much work on inviscid damping for other background flows and geometries. A broad collection of linear results has been obtained for various configurations, see for instance
[
lu2024singularity
,
ionescu2022linear
,
ren2025domain
,
ren2023linear
,
ren2025linear
,
BCZV19
,
CZZ19
,
GNRS20
,
Jia20
,
JiaGev20
,
WZZ18
,
WZZ19
,
WZZKolmo20
,
Zillinger16
,
Zillinger17
,
ZillingerCirc17
,
Zillinger21
]
. However, extending these results to the non-linear regime is notoriously difficult. The non-linear analysis in
[
BM15
]
required Gevrey regularity of the initial data to control the so-called “non-linear echoes” (transient growth of Fourier modes through non-linear interactions) as well as a delicate choice of coordinates. Non-linear inviscid damping for the Euler equations near monotone shear flows and point vortices was later established in
[
IJ22
,
IJ20
,
IJnon20
,
MZ20
]
, which remain the only known results for the full non-linear problem and also rely on Gevrey regularity. In contrast, initial data lacking such regularity can exhibit transient growth
[
deng2023long
]
or even fail to decay altogether
[
lin2011inviscid
,
sinambela2025transition
]
.

The studies
[
IJ22
,
IJnon20
,
MZ20
]
consider the periodic channel, where even at the linear level, the boundary behaviour of the perturbed vorticity is subtle, see
[
Zillinger16
,
lu2024singularity
]
. In this setting, the non-local terms can generate vorticity near the boundary that does not scatter with the background flow. To overcome this difficulty, it is typically assumed that both the perturbation
\omega^{0}
and the background curvature
v^{\prime\prime}
are compactly supported inside the channel, ensuring that the vorticity evolution reduces to pure transport near the boundaries and that compact support is preserved in time. We refer the reader to the surveys
[
ionescu2022nonlinear
,
wei2022hydro
]
for an in-depth discussion of inviscid damping phenomena in the Euler and equations.

The limiting absorption principle, central to the present work, has also been implemented in the periodic setting in
[
CZN23strip
]
, where explicit solutions to the linearised equations yield an alternative proof of damping for the velocity and density perturbations.

A particular sub-class of steady states of the Boussinesq system of the form (
1.2
) are the so-called
rest states
, corresponding to
v(y)\equiv 0
. For these equilibria, the stabilising effects of vorticity mixing are absent. Nevertheless, the stable stratification of the background density
\overline{\rho}
introduces dispersive effects that can extend the lifespan of solutions; see
[
elgindi2015sharp
,
jurja2024long
,
jurja2025effect
]
and references therein. When viscosity and thermal diffusivity are added to (
1.1
), the picture changes drastically: the equations become globally well-posed, and the vorticity mixing gives rise to an additional mechanism known as
enhanced dissipation
, which further stabilises the dynamics. We refer to the recent works
[
arbon2025enhanced
,
coti2024stability
,
knobel2025suppression
,
masmoudi2022stability
,
masmoudi2023asymptotic
,
niu2024improved
,
zhang2023stability
,
zhai2023stability
,
zillinger2021enhanced
,
masmoudi2025linear
]
for significant progress in this direction.

The two-dimensional Boussinesq equations are also closely related to the three-dimensional axisymmetric Euler equations away from the axis of symmetry. In that setting, finite-time singularities are known to occur, as demonstrated in the seminal work of Elgindi
[
Elgindi21
]
and further developed in
[
ElgindiJeong19
,
ElgindiJeong20
,
ChenHou23
]
. Conversely, inviscid damping plays a key role in establishing global well-posedness for the three-dimensional Euler equations and for the inhomogeneous two-dimensional Euler equations near certain stationary states, see
[
GPW
]
and
[
CWZZ
,
Zhao23
]
, respectively. In the context of the 2D Boussinesq equations near the stably stratified Couette flow, long but finite time existence has been obtained in
[
BBCZD21
]
using inviscid damping estimates. Whether this can be extended to global-in-time well-posedness, or whether instabilities eventually drive the system out of the perturbative regime, remains an intriguing open question.

In this direction, it is natural to ask whether the linear asymptotic stability of the stably stratified monotone shear flows (
1.2
) established in Theorem
B
persists for the full non-linear Boussinesq system, at least over long but finite times. A first step in this direction is to establish Gevrey regularity of the linear solutions. A second step is to determine whether the compact support of both the initial data and the background flow, together with the velocity estimates from Theorem
B
, suffices to guarantee that the vorticity remains compactly supported. These issues will be investigated in future work.

Finally, concerning the axisymmetric 3D Euler equations, it would be interesting to study the asymptotic stability of radial, pipe-like steady states where the velocity has axial and swirl components depending on the radial coordinate. These configurations constitute the closest analogue to the stably stratified shear flows (
1.2
) considered here. For such radial profiles, one can define an appropriate Richardson number, and a Miles–Howard-type stability criterion is available
[
howard1962hydrodynamic
]
. A complete description of the long-time linear dynamics near these steady states remains open. Motivated by the parallels between the two systems, we plan to explore whether the analytical framework and techniques developed in this paper can be adapted to the Euler setting in future work.

1.6.
Organization of the paper

In Section 2 we outline the strategy of the proof: we decouple the dynamics in Fourier modes, reduce the linearised problem to a family of (reduced) Taylor–Goldstein boundary value problems, and state the limiting absorption principle that underlies the time-decay effects. Sections
3
-
5
construct and analyse the Green’s function of the Reduced Taylor–Goldstein operator in the strong/weak (§
4
) and mild (§
5
) stratification regimes, isolating the regular and singular behaviours that determine the decay exponents. Section
6
develops mapping estimates for the associated solution and error operators in the tailored function spaces that capture the local structure near the critical layer. Section
7
builds homogeneous solutions adapted to these singular structures, and Section
8
settles the spectral picture of the linearised operator, proving the absence of discrete spectrum and establishing that the spectrum is purely continuous, thereby justifying the contour representations used later.

Sections
9
–
11
treat the regime-by-regime analysis required to pass from local resolvent control to global dynamics: we handle the “fragile” limit near the edges of stratification (Section
9
), prove the limiting absorption principle in the stratified region (Section
10
), and recover the Euler-type behaviour in the non-stratified region (Section
11
). Building on these ingredients, Section
12
derives Sobolev regularity for the spectral density and its good derivatives, which feeds into the oscillatory-phase arguments in Section
13
to obtain the inviscid damping estimates for velocity and density. Finally, Section
14
quantifies the sublinear growth of vorticity and density gradients. To conclude, the appendices contain a number of technical results that are used throughout the paper: properties of Whittaker functions, logarithmic approximations, formulas for Green’s functions, and higher-order operator bounds.

2.
Main ideas and sketch of the proof

In this section we rigorously present the arguments in the derivation of the decay rates, dividing the proof into several steps that are then completed in the corresponding sections of the manuscript. Throughout, we assume that hypotheses H
{\mathcal{P}}
, H
v
, and H1–H3 hold.

2.1.
Fourier decomposition and spectral representation

The setting of the periodic channel
{\mathbb{T}}\times[0,2]
considered in this article prevents the direct use of Fourier methods in the vertical direction
y
. However, we can still decouple (
1.3
)
in Fourier modes in
x\in{\mathbb{T}}
, writing

\displaystyle{\omega}=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\omega}_{k}(t,y){\rm e}^{ikx},\quad\rho=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\rho_{k}(t,y){\rm e}^{ikx},\qquad\psi=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\psi_{k}(t,y){\rm e}^{ikx},

so that

\begin{split}(\partial_{t}+ikv(y))\omega_{k}-ikv^{\prime\prime}(y)\psi_{k}&=-ik\mathfrak{g}\rho_{k},\\
(\partial_{t}+ikv(y))\rho_{k}&=ik\mathrm{P}(y)\psi_{k},\end{split}

(2.1)

for each
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, with

\begin{cases}{\Delta}_{k}\psi_{k}={\omega}_{k},\\
\psi_{k}|_{y=0,2}=0,\end{cases}\qquad{\Delta}_{k}:=\partial_{y}^{2}-k^{2}.

The modes corresponding to the
x
-average, that is
k=0
, are preserved by the time-evolution and thus we will not consider them further (cf. (
1.7
)).
Moreover, as
\omega
and
\rho
are real-valued functions, we necessarily have that
\overline{\omega_{-k}}=\omega_{k}
and
\overline{\rho_{-k}}=\rho_{k}
. Hence, without loss of generality, we take
k\geq 1
throughout the manuscript.

For our purposes, it is more convenient to write (
1.3
) in the compact stream-function formulation

\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{k}\\
\rho_{k}\end{pmatrix}+ikL_{k}\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{k}\\
\rho_{k}\end{pmatrix}=0,

and directly obtain its solution as

\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{k}\\
\rho_{k}\end{pmatrix}={\rm e}^{-ikL_{k}t}\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{k}^{0}\\
\rho_{k}^{0}\end{pmatrix}

where
L_{k}
is the linear operator defined by

L_{k}=\begin{pmatrix}{\Delta}_{k}^{-1}(v(y){\Delta}_{k}-v^{\prime\prime}(y))&\mathfrak{g}{\Delta}_{k}^{-1}\\
-\mathrm{P}(y)&v(y)\end{pmatrix}.

(2.2)

Using Dunford’s formula
[
Engel-Nagel
,
Taylor-11
]
, we have that

\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{k}(t,y)\\
\rho_{k}(t,y)\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\partial\Omega}{\rm e}^{-ikct}(c-L_{k})^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{k}^{0}(y)\\
\rho_{k}^{0}(y)\end{pmatrix}\,\mathrm{d}c,

(2.3)

where here
\Omega
is any domain containing the spectrum
\sigma(L_{k})
. Under the assumptions H1-H3, we have from Theorem
A
, see Theorem
2.4
for a more precise statement, that
\sigma(L_{k})=[v(0),v(2)]
, the range of the velocity field. Hence, we can reduce the contour of integration to

\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{k}(t,y)\\
\rho_{k}(t,y)\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\int_{0}^{2}{\rm e}^{-ikv(y_{0})t}\left((-v(y_{0})-i\varepsilon+L_{k})^{-1}-(-v(y_{0})+i\varepsilon+L_{k})^{-1}\right)\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{k}^{0}\\
\rho_{k}^{0}\end{pmatrix}\,v^{\prime}(y_{0})\mathrm{d}y_{0}.

(2.4)

For
\varepsilon>0
, we denote the resolvent pair

\begin{pmatrix}\psi^{\pm}_{k,\varepsilon}(y,y_{0})\\
\rho^{\pm}_{k,\varepsilon}(y,y_{0})\end{pmatrix}:=\left(-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon+L_{k}\right)^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{k}^{0}(y)\\
\rho_{k}^{0}(y)\end{pmatrix}

(2.5)

and obtain the coupled system of equations

\displaystyle{\omega}_{k}^{0}(y)

\displaystyle=(v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon){\Delta}_{k}\psi^{\pm}_{k,\varepsilon}(y,y_{0})-v^{\prime\prime}(y)\psi_{m,\varepsilon}^{\pm}+\mathfrak{g}\rho^{\pm}_{k,\varepsilon}(y,y_{0}),

\displaystyle\rho_{k}^{0}(y)

\displaystyle=(v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon)\rho^{\pm}_{k,\varepsilon}(y,y_{0})-\mathrm{P}(y)\psi^{\pm}_{k,\varepsilon}(y,y_{0}).

We first solve

\rho^{\pm}_{k,\varepsilon}(y,y_{0})=\frac{\rho_{k}^{0}(y)+\mathrm{P}(y)\psi^{\pm}_{k,\varepsilon}(y,y_{0})}{v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon}

(2.6)

and from there we obtain the following inhomogeneous
Taylor-Goldstein equation
for
\psi^{\pm}_{k,\varepsilon}
,

\begin{split}&\left(\Delta_{k}-\frac{v^{\prime\prime}(y)}{v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon}+\frac{{\mathcal{J}}(y)}{(v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon)^{2}}\right)\psi_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad=\frac{\omega_{k}^{0}(y)}{v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon}-\frac{\mathfrak{g}\rho_{k}^{0}(y)}{(v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon)^{2}},\end{split}

(TG)

along with homogeneous Dirichlet boundary conditions
\psi_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(0,y_{0})=\psi_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(2,y_{0})=0
, for all
y_{0}\in[0,2]
. Note how the local Richardson number enters the equation as the strength of the factor that becomes critically singular in the limit
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Motivated by the heuristic justifications in Section
1.4
, understanding the precise regularity properties of
\psi_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
and
\rho_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
is key for the application of oscillatory-phase arguments to (
2.4
). In this direction, we aim to study a simplified version of (
TG
) by reducing the operator into a fixed-coefficient operator.

2.2.
The Reduced Taylor-Goldstein operator and its Green’s function

We define the Reduced Taylor-Goldstein operator

\displaystyle\text{RTG}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}:=\partial_{y}^{2}-k^{2}+\frac{{\mathcal{J}}(y_{0})}{(y-y_{0}\pm i\varepsilon_{0})^{2}},\quad\varepsilon_{0}:=\frac{\varepsilon}{v^{\prime}(y_{0})}

(2.7)

and we further set the error operator

\displaystyle{\textsc{E}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}

\displaystyle:=-\frac{v^{\prime\prime}(y)}{v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon}+\frac{{\mathcal{P}}(y)-{\mathcal{P}}(y_{0})}{(v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon)^{2}}+\left(\frac{{\mathcal{P}}(y_{0})}{(v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon)^{2}}-\frac{{\mathcal{J}}(y_{0})}{(y-y_{0}\pm i\varepsilon_{0})^{2}}\right)

(2.8)

so that (
TG
) can be cast as

\displaystyle\text{RTG}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\psi_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})+{\textsc{E}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})\psi_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})=\frac{\omega_{k}^{0}(y)}{v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon}-\frac{\mathfrak{g}\rho_{k}^{0}(y)}{(v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon)^{2}}.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0},z)
denote the Green’s function of the Reduced Taylor-Goldstein operator with Dirichlett boundary conditions at
y=0
and
y=2
. It is such that

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{y}^{2}-k^{2}+\frac{{\mathcal{J}}(y_{0})}{(y-y_{0}\pm i\varepsilon_{0})^{2}}\right){\mathcal{G}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0},z)=\delta(y-z),\quad{\mathcal{G}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(0,y_{0},z)={\mathcal{G}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(2,y_{0},z)=0,

for all
y_{0},z\in[0,2]
.

To properly determine the regularity properties of
\psi_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
, a precise understanding of the Green’s function
{\mathcal{G}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
is most useful. We start by noting that for
y_{0}\in[0,\vartheta_{1}]\cup[\vartheta_{2},2]
we have
{\mathcal{J}}(y_{0})=v^{\prime\prime}(y_{0})=0
and the Reduced Taylor-Goldstein operator becomes the usual Laplacian operator, so that
{\mathcal{G}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0},z)
denotes the Green’s function of the Dirichlet Laplacian, which is studied in Section
11
.

For the most singular setting
y_{0}\in(\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2})
, we have
{\mathcal{J}}(y_{0})>0
and we construct the Green’s function with the standard method, first finding the homogeneous solutions to (
2.7
). We introduce here the regularity index

\displaystyle\gamma(y_{0})=\sqrt{\frac{1}{4}-{\mathcal{J}}(y_{0})}

(2.9)

for which we note that
\mu(y_{0})={\rm Re}(\gamma(y_{0}))
and we define
\nu(y_{0})={\rm Im}(\gamma(y_{0}))
. Fortunately,

\displaystyle M_{0,\gamma(y_{0})}((2k(y-y_{0}\pm i\varepsilon_{0})),\quad M_{0,-\gamma(y_{0})}((2k(y-y_{0}\pm i\varepsilon))

(2.10)

are two linearly independent homogeneous solutions to (
2.7
) for all
y_{0}\in(\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2})
with
{\mathcal{J}}(y_{0})\neq\frac{1}{4}
. Here,
M_{0,\gamma}
denotes the modified Whittaker function
[
Whittaker03
]
, and constitutes a central piece in our analysis. While we properly define and state its main properties in Appendix
B
, we record here that

\displaystyle M_{0,\gamma}(\zeta)=\zeta^{\frac{1}{2}+\gamma}\mathcal{E}_{0,\gamma}(\zeta),

(2.11)

where
\mathcal{E}_{0,\gamma}(\zeta)
is an analytic function, with
\mathcal{E}_{0,\gamma}(0)=1
and
\mathcal{E}_{0,\gamma}^{\prime}(0)=0
. Since
{\mathcal{G}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
is a suitable linear combination of the two homogeneous solutions (
2.10
), it follows that the regularity properties of
{\mathcal{G}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
are dictated by those of
M_{0,\gamma(y_{0})}
and
M_{0,-\gamma(y_{0})}
.

We next observe that for
y_{0}\in(0,2)
with
{\mathcal{J}}(y_{0})=\frac{1}{4}
, the two homogeneous solutions are no longer linearly independent, and a second pair of fundamental solutions must be considered. Without going into further details, which are relegated to the forthcoming sections, we further define

\displaystyle\widetilde{\varpi}_{1,1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\varpi_{1}-\varpi_{1,1}}{2},\quad\widetilde{\varpi}_{1,2}=\frac{\varpi_{1,2}-\varpi_{1}}{2}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\varpi}_{2,1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\varpi_{2}-\varpi_{2,1}}{2},\quad\widetilde{\varpi}_{2,2}=\frac{\varpi_{2,2}-\varpi_{2}}{2}

and we divide the interval
[0,2]
into four (intersecting) distinct regimes:

•

The non-stratified regime
I_{E}:=[0,\vartheta_{1}]\cup[\vartheta_{2},2]
, where
{\mathcal{J}}(y)=0
for all
y\in I_{E}
.

•

The weakly stratified regime
I_{W}:=(\vartheta_{1},\widetilde{\varpi}_{1,1})\cup(\widetilde{\varpi}_{2,2},\vartheta_{2})
. There holds
0<{\mathcal{J}}<\frac{1}{4}
in
I_{W}
.

•

The mildly stratified regime
I_{M}:=({\varpi}_{1,1},\varpi_{1,2})\cup({\varpi}_{2,1},\varpi_{2,2})\setminus\{\varpi_{1},\varpi_{2}\}
. There holds
\left|{\mathcal{J}}-\frac{1}{4}\right|\leq\tilde{\delta}
in
I_{M}
.

•

The strongly stratified regime
I_{S}:=(\widetilde{\varpi}_{1,2},\widetilde{\varpi}_{2,1})
. There holds
{\mathcal{J}}>\frac{1}{4}
in
I_{S}
.

Given the relevance of
\gamma(y_{0})
, to keep the notation as simple as possible, we set

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}:=\gamma(y_{0}),\quad\mu_{0}:=\mu(y_{0}),\quad\nu_{0}:=\nu(y_{0})

throughout the manuscript.

2.3.
Functional Spaces

To motivate the choice of our spaces, we first note that the meaningful scaling that balances out the two potential terms
-k^{2}
and
\frac{{\mathcal{J}}(y_{0})}{(y-y_{0}\pm i\varepsilon)^{2}}
in the Reduced Taylor-Goldstein operator (
2.7
) is roughly given by
k^{2}|y-y_{0}|^{2}\approx{\mathcal{J}}(y_{0})
. Hence, for
\beta^{2}:=\|{\mathcal{J}}\|_{L^{\infty}}>0
we define the
local
, resp.
non-local
sets

\displaystyle I_{n}(y_{0}):=\left\{y\in[0,2]\,:\,|y-y_{0}|\leq\frac{n\beta}{k}\right\},\quad I_{n}^{c}(y_{0}):=[0,2]\setminus I_{n}(y_{0})

for
n\geq 1
.

For the sake of clarity, we next argue for
y_{0}\in I_{E}\cup I_{S}
. Given the asymptotic expansion (
2.11
) for the homogeneous solutions to (
2.7
), we set

\displaystyle\eta=2k(y-y_{0}\pm i\varepsilon_{0})

and for functions
\varphi(y,y_{0})
we define

\displaystyle\|\varphi\|_{X_{k,\varepsilon,y_{0}}^{j}}:=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varphi_{\mathsf{r}},\varphi_{\mathsf{s}}\in H^{1}(I_{3}(y_{0}))\\
\varphi=\eta^{\frac{1}{2}+\gamma_{0}}\varphi_{\mathsf{r}}+\eta^{\frac{1}{2}-\gamma_{0}}\varphi_{\mathsf{s}}\end{subarray}}\sum_{n=0}^{j}k^{-n}\left(\|\eta^{-\frac{n}{2}}\partial_{y}^{n}\varphi_{\mathsf{r}}\|_{L^{\infty}(I_{3}(y_{0}))}+\|\eta^{-\frac{n}{2}}\partial_{y}^{n}\varphi_{\mathsf{s}}\|_{L^{\infty}(I_{3}(y_{0}))}\right).

and

\displaystyle\|\varphi(\cdot,y_{0})\|_{X_{k,\varepsilon,y_{0}}}:=\|\varphi\|_{X_{k}^{1}}+k^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\varphi(\cdot,y_{0})\|_{H^{1}_{k}(I_{3}^{c}(y_{0}))}.

Intuitively, the space
X_{k,\varepsilon,y_{0}}^{1}
measures the size of the coefficients
\varphi_{\mathsf{r}}
and
\varphi_{\mathsf{s}}
accompanying the
regular

\eta^{\frac{1}{2}+\gamma_{0}}
and
singular

\eta^{\frac{1}{2}-\gamma_{0}}
roots, respectively, in the scaling region
I_{3}(y_{0})
where the potential singularity is strongest.

While the space
X_{k,\varepsilon,y_{0}}
captures the regularity structures of the homogeneous solutions (
2.10
), the prescribed pointwise vanishing of
\partial_{y}\varphi_{\sigma}
is sometimes too restrictive, so we need to introduce a weaker space that nonetheless retains the most basic regularity properties. Indeed, we next define

\displaystyle\|\varphi(\cdot,y_{0})\|_{Z_{k,\varepsilon,y_{0}}}:=\|\varphi\|_{Z_{k,\varepsilon,y_{0}}^{1}}+k^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\varphi(\cdot,y_{0})\|_{H^{1}_{k}(I_{3}^{c}(y_{0}))}

where now

\displaystyle\|\varphi\|_{Z_{k,\varepsilon,y_{0}}^{1}}:=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varphi_{\mathsf{r}},\varphi_{\mathsf{s}}\in H^{1}(I_{3}(y_{0}))\\
\varphi=\eta^{\frac{1}{2}+\gamma_{0}}\varphi_{\mathsf{r}}+\eta^{\frac{1}{2}-\gamma_{0}}\varphi_{\mathsf{s}}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\Big(\|\varphi_{\mathsf{r}}\|_{L^{\infty}(I_{3}(y_{0}))}+\|\varphi_{\mathsf{s}}\|_{L^{\infty}(I_{3}(y_{0}))}

\displaystyle\quad+k^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|\partial_{y}\varphi_{\mathsf{r}}\|_{L^{2}(I_{3}(y_{0}))}+k^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|\partial_{y}\varphi_{\mathsf{s}}\|_{L^{2}(I_{3}(y_{0}))}\Big).

As usual, we set

\displaystyle X_{k,\varepsilon,y_{0}}:=\{\varphi\in L^{2}(0,2):\|\varphi\|_{X_{k,\varepsilon,y_{0}}}<+\infty\},\quad Z_{k,\varepsilon,y_{0}}:=\{\varphi\in L^{2}(0,2):\|\varphi\|_{Z_{k,\varepsilon,y_{0}}}<+\infty\}

and we readily note that
X_{k,\varepsilon,y_{0}}\subset Z_{k,\varepsilon,y_{0}}
since
\|\varphi\|_{Z_{k,\varepsilon,y_{0}}}\leq C\|\varphi\|_{X_{k,\varepsilon,y_{0}}}
, for some universal constant
C>0
independent of
k\geq 1
,
y_{0}\in[0,2]
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. To simplify notation, we shall omit the dependence on
\varepsilon
and
y_{0}
of the spaces, and just denote

\displaystyle X_{k}:=X_{k,\varepsilon,y_{0}},\quad Z_{k}:=Z_{k,\varepsilon,y_{0}}.

2.3.1.
The mild regime

When
y_{0}\in I_{M}
the mildly stratified region,
{\mathcal{J}}(y_{0})
is close to
\frac{1}{4}
, the associated
\gamma_{0}
is close to
0
and the Wronskian
{\mathcal{W}}\{\eta^{\frac{1}{2}+\gamma_{0}},\eta^{\frac{1}{2}-\gamma_{0}}\}=-4k\gamma_{0}
is also close to
0
, so that in the limit as
{\mathcal{J}}(y_{0})\rightarrow\frac{1}{4}
the two homogeneous solutions (
2.10
) lose their linear independence. To overcome this difficulty, we instead consider decompositions of the form

\varphi(y,y_{0})=\varphi_{\mathsf{r}}(y,y_{0})\eta^{\frac{1}{2}+\gamma_{0}}+\varphi_{\mathsf{s}}(y,y_{0})\eta^{\frac{1}{2}-\gamma_{0}}\log(\eta){\mathcal{Q}}_{\gamma_{0}}(\eta),

(2.12)

where

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\gamma_{0}}(\eta):=\int_{0}^{1}e^{2\gamma_{0}s\log(\eta)}\mathrm{d}s.

(2.13)

and for which
\mathcal{W}\{\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}+\gamma_{0}},\eta^{\frac{1}{2}-\gamma_{0}}\log(\eta)\mathcal{Q}_{\gamma}(\eta)\}=-2k
, which is always non-zero. Already simplifying the notation, we thus define

\displaystyle\|\varphi(\cdot,y_{0})\|_{LZ_{k}}:=\|\varphi\|_{LZ_{k}^{1}}+k^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\varphi(\cdot,y_{0})\|_{H^{1}_{k}(I_{3}^{c}(y_{0}))}

with

\displaystyle\|\varphi\|_{LZ_{k}^{j}}:=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varphi_{\mathsf{r}},\varphi_{\mathsf{s}}\in H^{1}(I_{3}(y_{0}))\\
\varphi=\eta^{\frac{1}{2}+\gamma_{0}}\varphi_{\mathsf{r}}+\eta^{\frac{1}{2}-\gamma_{0}}\log(\eta){\mathcal{Q}}_{\gamma_{0}}(\eta)\varphi_{\mathsf{s}}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\Big(\|\varphi_{\mathsf{r}}\|_{L^{\infty}(I_{3}(y_{0}))}+\|\varphi_{\mathsf{s}}\|_{L^{\infty}(I_{3}(y_{0}))}

\displaystyle\quad+\left.k^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|\partial_{y}\varphi_{\mathsf{r}}\|_{L^{2}(I_{3}(y_{0}))}+k^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|\partial_{y}\varphi_{\mathsf{s}}\|_{L^{2}(I_{3}(y_{0}))}\right)

and similarly

\displaystyle\|\varphi(\cdot,y_{0})\|_{LX_{k}}:=\|\varphi\|_{LX_{k}^{1}}+k^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\varphi(\cdot,y_{0})\|_{H^{1}_{k}(I_{3}^{c}(y_{0}))}

with

\displaystyle\|\varphi\|_{LX_{k}^{j}}:=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varphi_{\mathsf{r}},\varphi_{\mathsf{s}}\in H^{1}(I_{3}(y_{0}))\\
\varphi=\eta^{\frac{1}{2}+\gamma_{0}}\varphi_{\mathsf{r}}+\eta^{\frac{1}{2}-\gamma_{0}}\log(\eta){\mathcal{Q}}_{\gamma_{0}}(\eta)\varphi_{\mathsf{s}}\end{subarray}}\sum_{n=0}^{j}k^{-n}\left(\|\eta^{-\frac{n}{2}}\partial_{y}^{n}\varphi_{\mathsf{r}}\|_{L^{\infty}(I_{3}(y_{0}))}+\|\eta^{-\frac{n}{2}}\partial_{y}^{n}\varphi_{\mathsf{s}}\|_{L^{\infty}(I_{3}(y_{0}))}\right)

As before, there holds
\|\varphi\|_{LZ_{k}}\lesssim\|\varphi\|_{LX_{k}}
and thus
LX_{k}\subset LZ_{k}
.

2.4.
Regularity of the Green’s function, local to global bounds and regularization

As we previously indicated, we construct the Green’s function
{\mathcal{G}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
through the homogeneous solutions to (
2.7
). Since these homogeneous solutions (
2.10
) are fairly explicit, we can obtain explicit expressions for
{\mathcal{G}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
, that are recorded in Section
3
below. Crucially, the Green’s function inherits the regularity properties of these homogeneous solutions in the local region. In the weak and strong regimes, they are:

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
k\geq 1
,
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
y_{0}\in I_{S}\cup I_{W}
. There exists
\varepsilon_{*}>0
such that the Green’s function admits the decomposition

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0},z)=\left({\mathcal{G}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\right)_{\mathsf{r}}(y,y_{0},z)\eta^{\frac{1}{2}+\gamma_{0}}+\left({\mathcal{G}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\right)_{\mathsf{s}}(y,y_{0},z)\eta^{\frac{1}{2}-\gamma_{0}}

where
\eta=2k(y-y_{0}\pm i\varepsilon_{0})
,

\displaystyle\sup_{y\in I_{3}(y_{0})}\left\|\left({\mathcal{G}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\right)_{\sigma}(y,y_{0},\cdot)\right\|_{X_{k}^{1}}\lesssim k^{-1},\quad\sup_{y\in I_{3}(y_{0})}\left\|\left({\mathcal{G}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\right)_{\sigma}(y,y_{0},\cdot)\right\|_{H_{k}^{1}(I_{3}^{c}(y_{0}))}\lesssim k^{-\frac{3}{2}},

and

\displaystyle\sup_{y\in I_{3}(y_{0})}\left\|\partial_{y}\left({\mathcal{G}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\right)_{\sigma}(y,y_{0},\cdot)\right\|_{X_{k}^{1}}+\sup_{y\in I_{3}(y_{0})}k^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\|\partial_{y}\left({\mathcal{G}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\right)_{\sigma}(y,y_{0},\cdot)\right\|_{L^{2}(I_{3}^{c}(y_{0}))}\lesssim|\eta|

uniformly for all
y_{0}\in I_{S}\cup I_{W}
, all
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{*}
and
\sigma\in\{{\mathsf{r}},{\mathsf{s}}\}
.

Section
4
is devoted to the proof of the theorem for
y_{0}\in I_{S}\cup I_{W}
. An analogue of Theorem
2.1
for
LX_{k}
bounds on
{\mathcal{G}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0},z)
when
y_{0}\in I_{M}
is obtained in Section
5
.

2.4.1.
Local to global bounds

The above theorem is most useful to describe in
I_{3}(y_{0})
the solution
\Phi_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
to

\left({\Delta}_{k}+\frac{{\mathcal{J}}(y_{0})}{(y-y_{0}\pm i\varepsilon_{0})^{2}}\right){\Phi}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})=F_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})

(2.14)

with
\Phi_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(0,y_{0})=\Phi_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(2,y_{0})=0
, since it is given by

\displaystyle{\Phi}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}=\int_{0}^{2}{\mathcal{G}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0},z)F_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(z,y_{0})\mathrm{d}z

However, for
y\in I_{3}^{c}(y_{0})
, we cannot use Theorem
2.1
and we need a different way to estimate
\Phi_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
. Nevertheless, we can upgrade local
L^{2}(I_{2}^{c}(y_{0})\cap I_{3}(y_{0}))
bounds on
\Phi_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
to global
H_{k}^{1}(I_{3}^{c}(y_{0}))
estimates on
\Phi_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
thanks to the following inequality.

Lemma 2.2

(Entanglement Inequality – Lemma 7.1 in
[
CZN25chan
]
)
.

Let
\Phi_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
be a solution to (
2.14
), for some
F\in L^{2}(I_{2}^{c}(y_{0}))
. Then,

\displaystyle\|\Phi_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\|_{H_{k}^{1}(I_{3}^{c}(y_{0}))}\lesssim\|\Phi_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\|_{L^{2}(I_{2}^{c}(y_{0})\cap I_{3}(y_{0}))}+\frac{1}{k^{2}}\|F\|_{L^{2}(I_{2}^{c}(y_{0}))}

uniformly for all
k\geq 1
,
y_{0}\in[0,2]
and
\varepsilon>0
.

Similarly, while we may obtain pointwise bounds on
\Phi_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
for
y\in I_{3}(y_{0})
through Theorem
2.1
we also have the useful Sobolev-type inequality.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
f\in H^{1}_{k}(I_{3}(y_{0}))
. Then,
\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(I_{3}(y_{0}))}\lesssim k^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f\|_{H_{k}^{1}(I_{3}(y_{0}))}
.

Proof.

Let
z\in I_{3}(y_{0})
. Then,
f(z)=f(y)+\int_{y}^{z}f^{\prime}(s)\mathrm{d}s
, for all
y\in I_{3}(y_{0})
. In particular, integrating over
y\in I_{3}(y_{0})
, since
|I_{3}(y_{0})|\approx k^{-1}
, we reach

\displaystyle|f(z)|k^{-1}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{I_{3}(y_{0})}\left(|f(y)|+\left|\int_{y}^{z}f^{\prime}(s)\mathrm{d}s\right|\right)\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\lesssim k^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|f\|_{L^{2}(I_{3}(y_{0}))}+\|f^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(I_{3}(y_{0}))}\int_{I_{3}(y_{0})}|z-y|^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\lesssim k^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|f\|_{H_{k}^{1}(I_{3}(y_{0}))}

and the lemma follows.
∎

2.4.2.
Regularization of the source term

With the Green’s function
{\mathcal{G}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
at hand we observe that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2}{\mathcal{G}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0},z)\left(\frac{\omega_{k}^{0}(z)}{v(z)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon}-\frac{{\mathfrak{g}}\rho_{k}^{0}(y)}{(v(z)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon)^{2}}\right)\mathrm{d}z

should be finite for
\psi_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
to be well-defined. Thanks to Theorem
2.1
, we know that
{\mathcal{G}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0},z)
has two components, locally behaving like
\xi^{\frac{1}{2}+\gamma_{0}}
and
\xi^{\frac{1}{2}-\gamma_{0}}
, for
\xi=2k(z-y_{0}\pm i\varepsilon_{0})
. Therefore, while one hope to be able to estimate the vorticity integral uniformly in
\varepsilon>0
because
\xi^{-\frac{1}{2}\pm\gamma_{0}}\in L^{1}(I_{3}(y_{0}))
uniformly for all
\varepsilon>0
, one also notices that uniform boundedness of the density integral is much more subtle, since in general
\xi^{-\frac{3}{2}\pm\gamma_{0}}\in L^{1}(I_{3}(y_{0}))
for all
\varepsilon>0
but
\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\xi^{-\frac{3}{2}\pm\gamma_{0}}\not\in L^{1}(I_{3}(y_{0}))
.

In other words, the density term
\frac{{\mathfrak{g}}\rho_{k}^{0}(y)}{(v(z)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon)^{2}}
is a priori too singular to be handled by the Green’s function. This is to be expected, since it is in fact critically singular with respect to the Reduced Taylor-Goldstein equation and thus cannot be treated in a perturbative fashion. Nevertheless, since
\rho_{k}^{0}(y)=\mathrm{P}(y)\varrho_{k}^{0}(y)
, we define the regularized generalized stream-function

\displaystyle\varphi_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0}):={\psi}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})+\varrho_{k}^{0}(y)

(2.15)

which is such that

\left({\Delta}_{k}-\frac{v^{\prime\prime}(y)}{v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon}+\frac{{\mathcal{P}}(y)}{(v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon)^{2}}\right){\varphi}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}=\frac{w_{k}^{0}(y)}{v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon}+q_{k}^{0}(y)

(2.16)

with
\varphi_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(0,y_{0})=\varphi_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(2,y_{0})=0
where now

\displaystyle w_{k}^{0}(y):=\omega_{k}^{0}(y)-v^{\prime\prime}(y)\varrho_{k}^{0}(y),\quad q_{k}^{0}(y):=\Delta_{k}\varrho_{k}^{0}(y).

Hence, we can now make sense of the action of
{\mathcal{G}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
onto
q_{k}^{0}
if
\varrho_{k}^{0}
is sufficiently regular, a testament of the underlying motif in inviscid damping that decay costs regularity. That
\rho_{k}^{0}(y)=\mathrm{P}(y)\varrho_{k}^{0}(y)
is to consider density perturbations to take place only in the stratified region. See
[
Zhao23
]
for recent results on inviscid damping for the inhomogeneous Euler equations in the absence of buoyancy forces, namely
{\mathfrak{g}}=0
. While we do not address it here, the study of density perturbations in the background homogeneous Euler region under the effect of gravity forces is a very interesting open problem.

2.5.
The limiting absorption principle

With the help of the Green’s function
{\mathcal{G}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
, the solution
\varphi_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
to (
2.16
) is given by

\begin{split}\varphi_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})+\int_{0}^{2}{\mathcal{G}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0},z)&{\textsc{E}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(z,y_{0})\varphi_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(z,y_{0})\mathrm{d}z\\
&=\int_{0}^{2}{\mathcal{G}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0},z)\left(\frac{w_{k}^{0}(z)}{v(z)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon}+q_{k}^{0}(z)\right)\mathrm{d}z.\end{split}

(2.17)

To obtain estimates on the regularity structures of
\varphi_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
, we rely on the Limiting Absorption Principle, which consist on showing the existence of some
\kappa>0
independent of
k\geq 1
,
y_{0}\in I_{W}\cup I_{S}
and
\varepsilon>0
such that

\displaystyle\|\varphi_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\|_{Z_{k}}\leq\kappa\left\|\varphi_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})+\int_{0}^{2}{\mathcal{G}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0},z){\textsc{E}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(z,y_{0})\varphi_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(z,y_{0})\mathrm{d}z\right\|_{Z_{k}},

(2.18)

in order to use (
2.17
) to deduce estimates for
\varphi_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
in terms of the initial data
w_{k}^{0}
and
q_{k}^{0}
.

The existence of such
\kappa>0
is shown in Proposition
10.1
in Section
10
and involves a preliminary study on the mapping properties with respect to the
X_{k}
and
Z_{k}
spaces of the solution operators

\displaystyle T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}f(y):=\int_{0}^{2}{\mathcal{G}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0},z){\textsc{E}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})f(z)\mathrm{d}z

(2.19)

and

\displaystyle(R_{m,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}f)(y,y_{0}):=\int_{0}^{2}{\mathcal{G}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0},z)\frac{f(z)}{(v(z)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon)^{m}}\mathrm{d}z,

(2.20)

for
m\geq 0
, that are carried out in Section
6
and can be summarized to be

\displaystyle\|T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}f\|_{X_{k}}\lesssim k^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|f\|_{Z_{k}},\quad\|R_{1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}f\|_{X_{k}}\lesssim k^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|f\|_{H_{k}^{1}},\quad\|R_{0,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}f\|_{X_{k}}\lesssim k^{-\frac{3}{2}}\|f\|_{L^{2}},\quad

(2.21)

uniformly for all
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{*}
and all
y_{0}\in I_{S}\cup I_{W}
.

The proof of Proposition
10.1
argues by contradiction, essentially showing that (
2.18
) fails if we can find a sequence of parameters
k_{j}\geq 1
,
y_{j}\in I_{S}\cup I_{W}
such that
y_{j}\rightarrow y_{*}\in\overline{I_{S}\cup I_{W}}
,
\varepsilon_{j}\rightarrow 0^{+}
and
f_{j}\in Z_{k_{j}}
, with
\|f_{j}\|_{Z_{k_{j}}}=1
such that

\displaystyle\|f_{j}+T_{k_{j},\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}f_{j}(\cdot,y_{j})\|_{Z_{k_{j}}}\rightarrow 0

(2.22)

as
j\rightarrow\infty
. The estimate
\|T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}f\|_{X_{k}}\lesssim k^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|f\|_{Z_{k}}
shows that
|k_{j}|\lesssim 1
and hence
k_{j}\rightarrow k_{*}\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
up to a subsequence, so that
k_{j}\equiv k_{*}
for all
j
sufficiently large. In what follows, we already consider
j
large enough so that
k_{j}=k_{*}
. Moreover, for
g_{j}(y):=f_{j}(y)+T_{k_{*},\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}f_{j}(y,y_{j})
and
h_{j}(y)=f_{j}(y)-g_{j}(y)
we have
\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\|h_{j}\|_{Z_{k_{*}}}=1
and

\displaystyle h_{j}(y)+T_{k_{*},\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}h_{j}(y,y_{j})=-T_{k_{*},\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}g_{j}(y,y_{j})

(2.23)

with
h_{j}(0)=h_{j}(2)=0
. Equivalently, we obtain

\left({\Delta}_{k_{*}}-\frac{v^{\prime\prime}(y)}{v(y)-v(y_{j})\pm i\varepsilon_{j}}+\frac{{\mathcal{P}}(y)}{(v(y)-v(y_{j})\pm i\varepsilon_{j})^{2}}\right)h_{j}(y,y_{j})=-\textsc{E}_{k_{*},\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}g_{j}(y,y_{j}).

(2.24)

We then reach a contradiction with
\|h_{j}\|_{Z_{k_{*}}}\rightarrow 1
if we can show that the solution
h_{j}
to (
2.24
) converges to zero since
\|g_{j}\|_{Z_{k_{*}}}\rightarrow 0
, namely there are no embedded eigenvalues. In order to show that
h_{j}
does indeed vanish in
Z_{k_{*}}
, we need to solve the in-homogeneous Taylor-Goldstein equation (
2.24
) for some
k_{*}>1
fixed. To do so, we first study the
homogeneous
Taylor-Goldstein equation in Section
7
, where we find a pair of homogeneous solutions of the form

\begin{split}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})&:=(v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon)^{\frac{1}{2}+\gamma_{0}}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0}),\\
\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})&:=(v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon)^{\frac{1}{2}-\gamma_{0}}\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})\end{split}

(2.25)

for some smooth functions
\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
and
\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
, which closely resemble (
2.10
) that we later use to construct the in-homogeneous solution. These homogeneous solutions are also further used to study the spectral properties of the linearised operator
L_{k}
, see the next subsection for more details.

We observe here that since
y_{*}\in\overline{I_{S}\cup I_{W}}
we may have
y_{*}=\vartheta_{1}
or
y_{*}=\vartheta_{2}
, where there is change in the nature of the singularities of the Taylor-Goldstein operator, as
{\mathcal{P}}(y_{j})\rightarrow 0
the quadratic singularity vanishes, but the regularity of
\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})
may degenerate. Hence, in this
fragile
regime in which
{\mathcal{P}}(y_{j})\rightarrow 0
a careful study of the in-homogeneous solution
h_{j}
is carried out in Section
9
.

Likewise, we also note that
{\mathcal{P}}(y_{j})\rightarrow\frac{1}{4}
is a critical limit, in that just like for (
2.10
) the homogeneous solutions (
2.25
) also lose their linear independence and we instead need to find homogeneous solutions answering to a local decomposition of the form (
2.12
). The behaviour of the in-homogeneous solution
h_{j}
to (
2.24
) in this
mild
regime where
{\mathcal{P}}(y_{j})\rightarrow\frac{1}{4}
is considered in Section
7.2
.

We finally remark that one would actually like to have (
2.18
) in the stronger
X_{k}
space rather than
Z_{k}
. However, it is not straightforward to show that the in-homogeneous solution
h_{j}\in X_{k_{*}}
and
\|h_{j}\|_{X_{k_{*}}}\rightarrow 0
since, although similar to (
2.10
), the homogeneous solutions (
2.25
) may not share the exact same properties near the critical layer
y\approx y_{j}
. Nevertheless, once
\varphi_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\in Z_{k}
thanks to (
2.18
) and (
2.21
), we can use (
2.17
) and the regularizing properties of
T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
of (
2.21
) to conclude that actually
\varphi_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\in X_{k}
.

2.6.
Spectral theory of the linearised operator

A complete description of the spectrum of the linearised operator
L_{k}
is needed to establish the integral reduction (
2.4
) and the validity of limiting absorption principle encoded in (
2.18
). A more precise version of Theorem
A
reads

Theorem 2.4
.

Suppose that the background shear flow
v(y)
and stratified density
\overline{\rho}(y)
satisfy H
{\mathcal{P}}
, H
v
and H1–H3. Then,
L_{k}
