\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}:={\mathcal{T}}_{0}\circ{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm},\quad{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}:={\mathcal{T}}_{0}\circ{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}.

Let
y,y_{0}\in[0,2]
and
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{*}
. For
A\geq 1
and a function
f=f_{\varepsilon}(y,y_{0})
we define the space

\displaystyle\|f\|_{Y}:=\sup_{y\in[0,2]}\sup_{0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}}\left|\frac{f_{\varepsilon}(y,y_{0})}{\cosh(A(y-y_{0}))}\right|.

The next Lemma shows the mapping properties of
{\mathcal{T}}_{\sigma,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
with respect to the space
Y
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
k\geq 1
and
f\in Y
. Then,

\displaystyle\sup_{y_{0}\in[\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2}]}\|{\mathcal{T}}_{0}f\|_{Y}\leq\frac{\|f\|_{Y}}{A},\quad\sup_{y_{0}\in[\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2}]}\|{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}f\|_{Y}\lesssim\|f\|_{Y},

and hence
\sup_{y_{0}\in[\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2}]}\|{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}f\|_{Y}\lesssim\frac{\|f\|_{Y}}{A}
. Moreover, for
y_{0}\in(\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2})
we have

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}f\|_{Y}\lesssim\frac{\|f\|_{Y}}{1-2\gamma_{0}}

and thus
\|{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}f\|_{Y}\lesssim\frac{\|f\|_{Y}}{A(1-2\gamma_{0})}
.

Proof.

The estimate for
T_{0}
is straightforward, see
[
WZZ18
]
. We prove the estimate of
{\mathcal{T}}_{\sigma,1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
for
y_{0}\in I_{W}
, since for
y_{0}\in I_{S}
we have
\gamma_{0}\in i{\mathbb{R}}
and the proof is similar and easier. Firstly, for
\sigma={\mathsf{r}}
,

\displaystyle\left|\frac{{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}(y,y_{0})}{\cosh(A(y-y_{0}))}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\|f\|_{Y}}{\cosh(A(y-y_{0}))}\frac{1}{|v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon|}\int_{y_{0}}^{y}\cosh(A(z-y_{0}))\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\leq\|f\|_{Y}\frac{\tanh(A(y-y_{0}))}{A(y-y_{0})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{Y}

because
\|F_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon}(\cdot,y_{0})\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim 1
uniformly in
y_{0}
and
\varepsilon>0
and
|v(z)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon|\leq|v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon|
for all
z\in[y_{0},y]
since
v(\cdot)
is monotone increasing. Secondly, for
\sigma={\mathsf{s}}
we argue similarly, now integrating by parts once to obtain

\displaystyle\left|\frac{{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,\varepsilon}(y,y_{0})}{\cosh(A(y-y_{0}))}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\|f\|_{Y}}{\cosh(A(y-y_{0}))}\frac{1}{|v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon|^{1-2\gamma_{0}}}\int_{y_{0}}^{y}\cosh(A(z-y_{0}))(z-y_{0})^{-2\gamma_{0}}\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\|f\|_{Y}}{1-2\gamma_{0}}+\frac{\|f\|_{Y}}{\cosh(A(y-y_{0}))}\frac{1}{1-2\gamma_{0}}\int_{y_{0}}^{y}A\sinh(A(z-y_{0}))\frac{(z-y_{0})^{1-2\gamma_{0}}}{|v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon|}\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\|f\|_{Y}}{1-2\gamma_{0}}.

With this, the lemma is proved.
∎

Proposition 7.2
.

Let
y_{0}\in[\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2}]
and
\varepsilon>0
. Then there exist a unique solution
\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\in Y
to (
7.2
) such that

\displaystyle\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y_{0},y_{0})=1,\quad\partial_{y}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y_{0},y_{0})=0

with
\|\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\|_{Y}\leq C_{{\mathsf{r}}}
, for some constant
C_{{\mathsf{r}}}>0
independent of
y_{0}
and
\varepsilon>0
. Moreover, for
y_{0}\in(\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2})
there also exist a unique solution
\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\in Y
to (
7.3
) with

\displaystyle\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y_{0},y_{0})=1,\quad\partial_{y}\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y_{0},y_{0})=0

and
\|\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\|_{Y}\leq\frac{C_{{\mathsf{s}}}}{1-2\mu_{0}}
for some constant
C_{\mathsf{s}}>0
independent of
y_{0}
and
\varepsilon>0
.

Proof.

Integrating (
7.2
) and (
7.3
), we see that

\displaystyle\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})=1+k^{2}{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0}),\quad\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})=1+k^{2}{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0}).

(7.8)

Now, from Lemma
7.1
we know that
I-k^{2}{\mathcal{T}}_{\sigma,k,\varepsilon}
is an invertible operator in
Y
, for
A
large enough. Hence, we define

\displaystyle\phi_{\sigma,1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}:=(I-k^{2}{\mathcal{T}}_{\sigma,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm})^{-1}1\in Y,

(7.9)

with the bounds
\|\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})\|_{Y}\leq C_{\mathsf{r}}
uniformly for all
y_{0}\in[\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2}]
and
\|\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})\|_{Y}\leq\frac{C_{\mathsf{s}}}{1-2\gamma_{0}}
for all
y_{0}\in(\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2})
, for some
C_{\mathsf{r}},C_{\mathsf{s}}>0
.
∎

Once we have established the existence of
\phi_{\sigma,1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
and its uniform bounds in
Y
, we next state two fundamental properties that will be used throughout the manuscript.

Corollary 7.3
.

Let
k\geq 1
and
\varepsilon>0
. Then,

\displaystyle\sup_{y_{0}\in[\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2}]}\|\partial_{y}\phi_{\sigma,1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(\cdot,y_{0})\|_{L^{\infty}_{y}(0,2)}\lesssim_{k}1,

In particular,
\left|\phi_{\sigma,1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(\cdot,y_{0})-1\right|\lesssim_{k}{|y-y_{0}|}
for all
y_{0}\in[\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2}]
. Similarly, for
y_{0}\in(\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2})
there holds

\displaystyle\|\partial_{y}\phi_{\sigma,1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(\cdot,y_{0})\|_{L^{\infty}_{y}(0,2)}\lesssim_{k}\frac{1}{1-2\mu_{0}},

and thus
\left|\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(\cdot,y_{0})-1\right|\lesssim_{k}\frac{|y-y_{0}|}{1-2\mu_{0}}
as well.

Proof.

We note that
\partial_{y}\phi_{\sigma,1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}=k^{2}{\mathcal{T}}_{\sigma,1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\phi_{\sigma,1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
so that the result follows from Lemma
7.1
and Proposition
7.2
.
∎

7.1.
Continuity of the homogeneous solutions

Let
{\mathfrak{g}}>0
, we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{I}:=[0,2]\times(\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2})\times[0,1]\times(0,{\mathfrak{g}}],\quad\mathcal{Y}_{\overline{I}}:=[0,2]\times[\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2}]\times[0,1]\times[0,{\mathfrak{g}}],

we address the continuity of the solutions
\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})
and
\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})
in the spaces
\mathcal{Y}_{I}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{\overline{I}}
, respectively, where we further record the dependence on the physical gravity
{\mathfrak{g}}>0
.

Proposition 7.4
.

Let
k\geq 1
. Then,

\displaystyle\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k}^{\pm}(y,y_{0},\varepsilon,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}):=\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})\in C(\mathcal{Y}_{\overline{I}})

and

\displaystyle\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k}^{\pm}(y,y_{0},\varepsilon,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}):=\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,\varepsilon,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})\in C(\mathcal{Y}_{{I}})

Proof.

We note that

\displaystyle\phi_{\sigma,1,k,\varepsilon,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}}^{\pm}:=(I-k^{2}{\mathcal{T}}_{\sigma,k,\varepsilon,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}}^{\pm})^{-1}1=\sum_{n\geq 0}\left(k^{2}{\mathcal{T}}_{\sigma,k,\varepsilon,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}}^{\pm}\right)^{n}1,

where convergence is uniform. Hence, the lemma follows if we show that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{r}},k}^{\pm}:={\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}}^{\pm}:C(\mathcal{Y}_{\overline{I}})\rightarrow C(\mathcal{Y}_{\overline{I}})\quad\text{ and }\quad{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{s}},k}^{\pm}:={\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}}^{\pm}:C(\mathcal{Y}_{{I}})\rightarrow C(\mathcal{Y}_{{I}}).

To that purpose, we first argue for
{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{r}},k}^{\pm}
, with
y_{0}\in[\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2}]
,
\varepsilon\in[0,1]
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}\geq 0
and we observe that

\left({\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}}^{\pm}f\right)(y,y_{0})=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\left(\mathcal{K}_{0,{\mathsf{r}},\varepsilon,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}}^{\pm}(s,t,y,y_{0})+\mathcal{K}_{1,{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}}(s,t,y,y_{0})\right)f(y_{0}+st(y-y_{0}),y_{0})\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}t,

where

\mathcal{K}_{0,{\mathsf{r}},\varepsilon,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}}^{\pm}(s,t,y,y_{0}):=t(y-y_{0})^{2}\left(\frac{v(y_{0}+st(y-y_{0}))-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon}{v(y_{0}+t(y-y_{0}))-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon}\right)^{1+2\gamma_{0}(\tilde{\mathfrak{g}})}

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{1,{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}}(s,t,y,y_{0})

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{K}_{2,{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}}^{\pm}(s,y,y,y_{0})\left(\frac{v(y_{0}+st(y-y_{0}))-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon}{v(y_{0}+t(y-y_{0}))-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon}\right)^{2\gamma_{0}(\tilde{\mathfrak{g}})}

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{2,{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}}(s,t,y,y_{0})

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{k^{2}}\frac{t(y-y_{0})^{2}v^{\prime\prime}(y_{0}+st(y-y_{0}))}{v(y_{0}+t(y-y_{0}))-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon}\left(\frac{1}{2}-\gamma_{0}(\tilde{\mathfrak{g}})\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{k^{2}}\frac{t(y-y_{0})^{2}}{v(y_{0}+t(y-y_{0}))-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon}\frac{\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}(y_{0}+st(y-y_{0}))-\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}(y_{0})}{v(y_{0}+st(y-y_{0}))-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon}.

In particular, we easily see that

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{K}_{n,{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}}(s,t,y,y_{0})\right|\lesssim 1,

for
n=0,1,2
, uniformly for all
(y,y_{0},\varepsilon,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}})\in\mathcal{Y}_{\overline{I}}
and
s,t\in(0,1)
. Hence, by the Dominated Convergence Theorem we see that
{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{r}},k}^{\pm}
maps
C(\mathcal{Y}_{\overline{I}})
to itself.

Regarding
{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{s}},k}^{\pm}
, we define
\mathcal{K}_{n,{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon,{\mathfrak{g}}}
accordingly. We note that
\left|\mathcal{K}_{2,{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}}(s,t,y,y_{0})\right|\lesssim t\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}|y-y_{0}|
, so that

\displaystyle t|y-y_{0}|\int_{0}^{1}\left|\frac{v(y_{0}+st(y-y_{0}))-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon}{v(y_{0}+t(y-y_{0}))-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon}\right|^{-2\gamma_{0}(\tilde{\mathfrak{g}})}\mathrm{d}s\lesssim(t|y-y_{0}|)^{1-2\gamma_{0}(\tilde{\mathfrak{g}})}\lesssim 1\in L^{1}_{t}(0,1)

and thus we can use the Dominated Convergence Theorem for the term involving
\mathcal{K}_{1,{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}}(s,t,y,y_{0})
. On the other hand, we still have
\left|\mathcal{K}_{0,{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}}(s,t,y,y_{0})\right|\lesssim 1
and thus we can use the Dominated Convergence Theorem for this contribution as well. With this, the proof is concluded.
∎

7.2.
Homogeneous solutions in the mild regime

The two homogeneous solutions
\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
and
\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
have Wronskian
\mathcal{W}\{\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(\cdot,y_{0}),\,\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(\cdot,y_{0})\}=2\gamma_{0}v^{\prime}(y_{0})
and thus they lose its linear independence as
\gamma_{0}\rightarrow 0
. Hence, they do not constitute a suitable pair of fundamental solutions with which we can construct solutions to the in-homogeneous equation. Instead, for
y_{0}\neq\varpi_{1},\varpi_{2}
, we define

\phi_{\mathrm{L},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0}):=\frac{\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})-\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})}{2\gamma_{0}}

(7.10)

for which a simple computation shows that now
\mathcal{W}\{\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(\cdot,y_{0}),\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(\cdot,y_{0})\}=v^{\prime}(y_{0})
, for all
y_{0}\in I_{M}
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})

\displaystyle=(v(y)-v(y_{0})+i\varepsilon)^{\frac{1}{2}-\gamma_{0}}\frac{(v(y)-v(y_{0})+i\varepsilon)^{2\gamma_{0}}-1}{2\gamma_{0}}\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})

\displaystyle\quad+(v(y)-v(y_{0})+i\varepsilon)^{\frac{1}{2}+\gamma_{0}}\frac{\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,c)-\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})}{2\gamma_{0}},

and for

\displaystyle\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0}):=\frac{\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,c)-\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})}{2\gamma_{0}},

we have that

\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})=k^{2}{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})+k^{2}\left(\frac{{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}-T_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}}{2\gamma_{0}}\right)\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}(y,y_{0})

(7.11)

and thus we can write

\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})=k^{2}\left(I-k^{2}{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}-T_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}}{2\gamma_{0}}\right)\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})

(7.12)

The following results shows the existence of
\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
.

Proposition 7.5
.

Let
y_{0}\in I_{M}
and
\varepsilon>0
. Then there exists a solution
\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\in Y
to (
7.11
) with
\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y_{0},y_{0})=1
and
\partial_{y}\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y_{0},y_{0})=0
such that
\|\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\|_{Y}\leq C
, for some constant
C>0
independent of
y_{0}
and
\varepsilon>0
.

Proof.

The proposition follows from Proposition
7.2
once we show that

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\frac{{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}-T_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}}{2\gamma_{0}}\right)\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\right\|_{Y}\lesssim A^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\|_{Y}

for some implicit constant independent of
y_{0}\in I_{M}
and
\varepsilon>0
. To that end, recalling (
7.6
) and (
7.7
), together with (
7.4
) and (
7.5
), we have

\begin{split}&\left(\frac{{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}-T_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}}{2\gamma_{0}}\right)\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})\\
&=\frac{1}{v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon}\int_{y_{0}}^{y}\mathrm{F}_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})\left(\frac{\left(\frac{v(z)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon}{v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon}\right)^{2\gamma_{0}}-\left(\frac{v(z)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon}{v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon}\right)^{-2\gamma_{0}}}{2\gamma_{0}}\right)\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(z,y_{0})\mathrm{d}z\\
&\quad-\frac{1}{k^{2}(v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon)}\int_{y_{0}}^{y}v^{\prime\prime}(z)\left(\frac{v(z)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon}{v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon}\right)^{-2\gamma_{0}}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(z,y_{0})\mathrm{d}z.\end{split}

(7.13)

Moreover,

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\left(\frac{v(z)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon}{v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon}\right)^{2\gamma_{0}}-\left(\frac{v(z)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon}{v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon}\right)^{-2\gamma_{0}}}{2\gamma_{0}}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim|z-y_{0}|^{-2\gamma_{0}}|v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon|^{2\gamma_{0}}|\log(A|z-y_{0}|)|

\displaystyle\quad+|z-y_{0}|^{-2\gamma_{0}}|v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon|^{2\gamma_{0}}|\log(A|y-y_{0}|)|.

Since
|1-2\gamma_{0}|
is uniformly bounded away from zero for
y_{0}\in I_{M}
, there holds

\displaystyle\left|\int_{y_{0}}^{z}\cosh(A(s-y_{0}))|s-y_{0}|^{-2\gamma_{0}}|\log|s-y_{0}||\mathrm{d}s\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\cosh(A(z-y_{0}))|z-y_{0}|^{1-2\gamma_{0}}|\log(A|z-y_{0}|)|

\displaystyle\quad+\left|\int_{y_{0}}^{z}\cosh(A(s-y_{0}))|s-y_{0}|^{-2\gamma_{0}}\mathrm{d}s\right|

\displaystyle\quad+\left|A\int_{y_{0}}^{z}\sinh(A(s-y_{0}))\mathrm{d}s\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\cosh(A(z-y_{0}))|z-y_{0}|^{1-2\gamma_{0}}\left(1+|\log(A|z-y_{0}|)\right)

and

\displaystyle\left|\int_{y_{0}}^{z}\cosh(A(s-y_{0}))|s-y_{0}|^{-2\gamma_{0}}\mathrm{d}s\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\cosh(A(z-y_{0}))|z-y_{0}|^{1-2\gamma_{0}}

\displaystyle\quad+A\int_{y_{0}}^{z}\sinh(A(s-y_{0}))|s-y_{0}|^{1-2\gamma_{0}}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\lesssim\cosh(A(z-y_{0}))|z-y_{0}|^{1-2\gamma_{0}},

so that now

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\left(\frac{{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}-T_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}}{2\gamma_{0}}\right)\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})}{\cosh(A(y-y_{0}))}\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\lesssim\frac{\|\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\|_{Y}}{\cosh(A(y-y_{0}))}\int_{y_{0}}^{y}\frac{\cosh(A(z-y_{0}))|z-y_{0}|^{1-2\gamma_{0}}}{|v(z)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon|^{1-2\gamma_{0}}}\left(1+|\log(A|z-y_{0}|)\right)\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\qquad\lesssim\|\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\|_{Y}\frac{\tanh(A(y-y_{0}))}{A}\left(1+|\log(A|z-y_{0}|)|\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\|\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\|_{Y}}{\cosh(A(z-y_{0}))}\int_{y_{0}}^{y}\frac{\tanh(A(z-y_{0}))}{A(z-y_{0})}\cosh(A(z-y_{0}))\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\qquad\lesssim\|\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\|_{Y}\frac{\tanh(A(y-y_{0}))}{A}\left(1+|\log(A|z-y_{0}|)|\right),

for all
y\in[0,2]
and all
y_{0}\in I_{M}
and
\varepsilon>0
. Further observing that
|\tanh(\zeta)\log|\zeta||\lesssim 1
for
|\zeta|\leq 10
,
|\zeta^{-\frac{1}{2}}|\log|\zeta||\lesssim 1
for
|\zeta|>9
and
\tanh(\zeta)
is uniformly bounded, we conclude that

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\frac{{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}-T_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}}{2\gamma_{0}}\right)\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})\right\|_{Y}\lesssim\frac{1}{A^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\|_{Y}

and hence we can proceed as in Proposition
7.2
to show the existence and the uniform bounds in
Y
of the solution
\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
to (
7.11
). With this, the proof is finished.
∎

We next study the homogeneous solutions in the critical point
y_{0}=\varpi_{1}
or
y_{0}=\varpi_{2}
, for which
{\mathcal{J}}(y_{0})=\frac{1}{4}
and
\gamma_{0}=0
. While
\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})
is still well defined,
\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})
as given in (
7.10
) is not so. However, for all
y,y_{0}\in[0,2]
, we now set

\begin{split}\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})&:=(v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon)^{\frac{1}{2}}\log(v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon)\phi_{0,1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})\\
&\quad+(v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon)^{\frac{1}{2}}\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0}),\end{split}

(7.14)

where

\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})=k^{2}{\mathcal{T}}_{0,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})+k^{2}{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathrm{L}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\phi_{0,1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})

(7.15)

with

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathrm{L}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}={\mathcal{T}}_{0}\circ{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm},\quad{\mathcal{T}}_{0,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}={\mathcal{T}}_{0}\circ{\mathcal{T}}_{0,k,1,\varepsilon}^{\pm},

where

\begin{split}{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}f(y,y_{0})&=\frac{2}{v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon}\int_{y_{0}}^{y}\mathrm{F}_{0,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(z,y_{0})\log\left(\frac{v(z)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon}{v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon}\right)f(z,y_{0})\mathrm{d}z\\
&\quad-\frac{1}{k^{2}}\frac{1}{v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon}\int_{y_{0}}^{y}v^{\prime\prime}(z)f(z,y_{0})\mathrm{d}z.\end{split}

(7.16)

and

\begin{split}{\mathcal{T}}_{0,1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}f(y,y_{0})&=\frac{1}{v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon}\int_{y_{0}}^{y}\mathrm{F}_{0,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(z,y_{0})f(z,y_{0})\mathrm{d}z\end{split}

(7.17)

where we now denote

\displaystyle\mathrm{F}_{0,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0}):=(v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon)-\frac{(v^{\prime}(y))^{2}}{k^{2}}\frac{{\mathcal{J}}(y)-\frac{1}{4}}{(v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon)^{2}}

(7.18)

and
\phi_{0,1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})
is the unique solution to
\phi_{0,1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})=1+k^{2}{\mathcal{T}}_{0,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\phi_{0,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})
arguing as in Proposition
7.2
The existence, regularity and continuity in
\varepsilon\in[0,1]
of
\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})
follows from the Neumann series’ representation of Proposition
7.2
once it is shown that

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathrm{L}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}f(y,y_{0})\|_{Y}\lesssim A^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|f\|_{Y},

which is obtained arguing as in the proof of Proposition
7.5
. Hence, we have

Proposition 7.6
.

Let
k\geq 1
and
\varepsilon>0
. There exists a unique solution
\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})\in Y
to (
7.15
) with

\displaystyle\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y_{0},y_{0})=1,\quad\partial_{y}\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y_{0},y_{0})=0

and such that
\|\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})\|_{Y}\leq C
, for some constant
C>0
independent and
\varepsilon>0
. Furthermore,
\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,\varepsilon}(y,y_{0})
is continuous for all
(y,y_{0},\varepsilon)\in[0,2]\times[\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2}]\times[0,1]
.

Proof.

We shall only provide some details about the continuity statement. From (
7.11
), we observe that we shall only show that
{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathrm{L}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
maps
C([0,2]\times[\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2}]\times[0,1])
to itself, since
{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
already does so thanks to Proposition
7.4
and
\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})\in C([0,2]\times[\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2}]\times[0,1])
. Arguing as in the proof of Proposition
7.4
we see with the dominated convergence theorem that
{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathrm{L}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
maps
C([0,2]\times[\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2}]\times[0,1])
to itself as well.
∎

By the definition of
\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
, the usual computation shows that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{W}}\{\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(\cdot,y_{0}),\,\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(\cdot,y_{0})\}=v^{\prime}(y_{0})\neq 0

for all
\varepsilon>0
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{y}-k^{2}-\frac{v^{\prime\prime}(y)}{v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon}+\frac{{\mathcal{P}}(y)}{(v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon)^{2}}\right)\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})=0.

Indeed, there holds

\displaystyle\textsc{TG}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}

\displaystyle\left((v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon)^{\frac{1}{2}}\log(v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon)\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{v^{\prime\prime}(y)}{(v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})+2\frac{v^{\prime}(y)}{(v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon)^{\frac{1}{2}}}k^{2}{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})

\displaystyle=-\textsc{TG}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}\left((v(y)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon)^{\frac{1}{2}}\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})\right).

Hence,
\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})
and
\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})
constitute a linearly independent pair of homogeneous solutions to the Taylor-Goldstein equation for all
y_{0}\in(\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2})
for which
{\mathcal{J}}(y_{0})=\frac{1}{4}
.

8.
Spectrum of the Linearised Operator

In this section we characterize the spectrum of the linearised operator
L_{k}
defined in (
2.2
). We consider the operator
L_{k}
in the domain of definition

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(L_{k})=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}f\\
g\end{pmatrix}\,:\,f\in H^{2}(0,2)\cap H^{1}_{0}(0,2),\quad g\in L^{2}(0,2),\quad\text{supp }g,\,\text{supp }\Delta_{k}f\subseteq(\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2})\right\}.

Since
\text{supp }v^{\prime\prime},\,\text{supp }{\mathcal{P}}\subseteq(\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2})
, it is immediate to see that

\displaystyle L_{k}:\mathcal{D}(L_{k})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(L_{k})

continuously. As usual, we say that
\lambda\in\sigma(L_{k})\subset{\mathbb{C}}
if
(L_{k}-\lambda)
is not continuously invertible. The main goal of this section is to prove Theorem
2.4
, namely that
\sigma(L_{k})=[v(\vartheta_{1}),v(\vartheta_{2})]
, through a combination of several results. First, we write

\displaystyle L_{k}=\widetilde{L}_{k}+\mathcal{K}_{k},\quad\widetilde{L}_{k}:=\begin{pmatrix}v(y)&0\\
-\mathrm{P}(y)&v(y)\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathcal{K}_{k}:=\begin{pmatrix}\Delta_{k}^{-1}\left([v,\Delta_{k}]-v^{\prime\prime}(y)\right)&{\mathfrak{g}}\Delta_{k}^{-1}\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}

and we note that
\mathcal{K}_{k}
is a compact perturbation of
\widetilde{L}_{k}
. Moreover, we easily see that
[v(\vartheta_{1}),v(\vartheta_{2})]\subseteq\sigma_{ess}(\widetilde{L}_{k})
since
(\widetilde{L}_{k}-\lambda)
is not surjective for all
\lambda\in[v(\vartheta_{1}),v(\vartheta_{2})]
. We then conclude that
[v(\vartheta_{1}),v(\vartheta_{2})]\subseteq\sigma(L_{k})
. The rest of the section is devoted to showing that
L_{k}-\lambda
is invertible for all
\lambda\not\in[v(\vartheta_{1}),v(\vartheta_{2})]
and that no
\lambda\in[v(\vartheta_{1}),v(\vartheta_{2})]
is an embedded eigenvalue of
L_{k}
.

8.1.
The non-stratified region

Firstly, we investigate those
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
such that
{\rm Re}(\lambda)\not\in(\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2})=\text{supp }{\mathcal{P}}
.

Proposition 8.1
.

Let
v(y)
and
{\mathcal{P}}(y)
satisfy H
{\mathcal{P}}
, H
v
and
H1
. Let
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
such that
{\rm Re}(\lambda)\not\in[v(0),v(2)]
. Then,
\lambda\not\in\sigma(L_{k})
.

Proof.

To show that
\lambda
is in the resolvent of
L_{k}
, we shall see that for any pair
\begin{pmatrix}f\\
g\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{D}(L_{k})
we can find a pair
\begin{pmatrix}\psi\\
\rho\end{pmatrix}\in D(L_{k})
such that

\left(L_{k}-\lambda\right)\begin{pmatrix}\psi\\
\rho\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}f\\
g\end{pmatrix}

(8.1)

with

\|\psi\|_{H^{2}(0,2)\cap H^{1}_{0}(0,2)}+\|\rho\|_{L^{2}(0,2)}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{2}(0,2)}+\|g\|_{L^{2}(0,2)}.

(8.2)

By definition of
L_{k}
, we find

\rho(y)=\frac{g(y)+{\mathcal{P}}(y)\psi(y)}{v(y)-\lambda},

(8.3)

where
\psi(y)
is a solution to

\partial_{y}^{2}\psi(y)-k^{2}\psi(y)-\frac{v^{\prime\prime}(y)}{v(y)-\lambda}\psi(y)+\frac{{\mathcal{P}}(y)}{(v(y)-\lambda)^{2}}\psi(y)=\frac{h(y)}{v(y)-\lambda}-\frac{{\mathfrak{g}}g(y)}{(v(y)-\lambda)^{2}}

(8.4)

with boundary conditions
\psi(0)=\psi(2)=0
and where we denote
h(y)=\Delta_{k}f(y)
. Firstly, since
{\rm Re}(\lambda)\not\in[v(0),v(2)]
and
\text{supp }v^{\prime\prime},\,\text{supp }{\mathcal{P}},\,\text{supp }h,\,\text{supp }g\subseteq(\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2})
, we see that
\psi\in H^{2}(0,2)\cap H^{1}_{0}(0,2)
is a classical solution to the equation, with
\omega=\Delta_{k}\psi
having its support contained in
(\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2})
. Hence,
\rho\in L^{2}(0,2)
and
\text{supp }\rho\in(\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2})
as well. Now, in order to show the continuity of
(L_{k}-\lambda)^{-1}:\mathcal{D}(L_{k})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(L_{k})
, we
multiply by
\overline{\psi}
and integrate by parts to see that

\int_{0}^{2}|\partial_{y}\psi|^{2}+k^{2}|\psi|^{2}+\frac{v^{\prime\prime}(y)}{v(y)-\lambda}|\psi|^{2}-\int_{0}^{2}P(y)\frac{|\psi|^{2}}{(v(y)-\lambda)^{2}}=-\int_{0}^{2}\frac{h(y)\overline{\psi(y)}}{v(y)-\lambda}\mathrm{d}y+{\mathfrak{g}}\int_{0}^{2}\frac{g(y)\overline{\psi(y)}}{(v(y)-\lambda)^{2}}\mathrm{d}y

(8.5)

Now, since
{\rm Re}(\lambda)\not\in[v(0),v(2)]
, say
{\rm Re}(\lambda)<v(0)
so that there holds

\left|\int_{0}^{2}\frac{v^{\prime\prime}(y)}{|v(y)-\lambda|}|\psi(y)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}y\right|\leq\int_{0}^{2}\frac{|v^{\prime\prime}(y)|}{v(y)-v(0)}|\psi(y)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}y\leq\frac{\|v^{\prime\prime\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}}}{c_{0}}\|\psi\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

with also

\left|\int_{0}^{2}{\mathcal{P}}(y)\frac{|\psi(y)|^{2}}{(v(y)-\lambda)^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}y\right|\leq\frac{\|{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}}}{2c_{0}^{2}}\|\psi\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

We define
\epsilon:=\frac{\|v^{\prime\prime\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}}}{c_{0}}+\frac{\|{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}}}{2c_{0}^{2}}<1
. On the other hand, since
\text{supp }h,\,\text{supp }g\in(\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2})
, for
\vartheta=\min(\vartheta_{1},2-\vartheta_{2})
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{2}\frac{h(y)\overline{\psi}(y)}{v(y)-\lambda}\mathrm{d}y\right|\leq\int_{0}^{2}\frac{|h(y)|\psi(y)|}{v(y)-v(0)}\mathrm{d}y\leq\frac{1}{c_{0}\vartheta}\int_{\vartheta_{1}}^{\vartheta_{2}}|h(y)||\psi(y)|\leq C_{\vartheta,\epsilon}\|h\|_{L^{2}(0,2)}^{2}+\frac{1-\epsilon}{4}\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(0,2)}^{2}

and similarly

\displaystyle\left|{\mathfrak{g}}\int_{0}^{2}\frac{g(y)\overline{\psi}(y)}{(v(y)-\lambda)^{2}}\mathrm{d}y\right|\leq C_{\vartheta,\epsilon}\|g\|_{L^{2}(0,2)}^{2}+\frac{1-\epsilon}{4}\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(0,2)}^{2}

Thus, we conclude that

\displaystyle\frac{1-\epsilon}{2}\left(\int_{0}^{2}\left(|\partial_{y}\psi(y)|^{2}+k^{2}|\psi(y)|^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}y\right)\leq C_{\vartheta,\epsilon}\left(\|h\|_{L^{2}(0,2)}^{2}+\|g\|_{L^{2}(0,2)}^{2}\right).

For
{\rm Re}(\lambda)>v(2)
we argue similarly, and the lemma follows.
∎

The next results shows that under H1 the spectrum does not include the range of the shear flow restricted to the non-stratified region.

Proposition 8.2
.

Assume that
v(y)
and
{\mathcal{P}}(y)
satisfy H
{\mathcal{P}}
, H
v
and H1. Let
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
such that
{\rm Re}(\lambda)\not\in[v(\vartheta_{1}),v(\vartheta_{2})]
. Then,
\lambda\not\in\sigma(L_{k})
.

Proof.

We argue as in the proof of Proposition
8.1
, so that for any pair
\begin{pmatrix}f\\
g\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{D}(L_{k})
we can find a pair
\begin{pmatrix}\psi\\
\rho\end{pmatrix}\in D(L_{k})
such that (
8.1
) holds. As before,
\psi(y)
is given by (
8.4
) and
\rho(y)
is given by (
8.3
). Thanks to the support assumptions on
v^{\prime\prime}
,
{\mathcal{P}}
,
h
and
g
, we see that
\psi
is a classical solution to (
8.4
) and that
\begin{pmatrix}\psi\\
\rho\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{D}(L_{k})
. To prove (
8.2
), we now note that for
{\rm Re}(\lambda)\in[v(0),v(\vartheta_{1}))
, there is a unique
y_{\lambda}\in[0,\vartheta_{1})
such that
v(y_{\lambda})={\rm Re}(\lambda)
. Moreover,

\left|\int_{0}^{2}{\mathcal{P}}(y)\frac{|\psi(y)|^{2}}{(v(y)-\lambda)^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}y\right|\leq\int_{\vartheta_{1}}^{\vartheta_{2}}{\mathcal{P}}(y)\frac{|\psi(y)|^{2}}{(v(y)-v(y_{\lambda}))^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}y\leq\frac{\|{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}}}{2c_{0}^{2}}\|\psi\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

and

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{2}\frac{v^{\prime\prime}(y)}{v(y)-\lambda}|\psi(y)|^{2}\mathrm{d}y\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\vartheta_{1}}^{\vartheta_{2}}\frac{|v^{\prime\prime}(y)|}{v(y)-v(y_{\lambda})}|\psi(y)|^{2}\mathrm{d}y\leq\frac{\|v^{\prime\prime\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,2)}}{c_{0}}\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(0,2)}^{2}

for which we note that

\displaystyle\epsilon_{1}:=\frac{\|{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}}}{2c_{0}^{2}}+\frac{\|v^{\prime\prime\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,2)}}{c_{0}}<1.

On the other hand, since
\text{supp }h,\,\text{supp }g\subseteq[\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2}]
and
y_{\lambda}<\vartheta_{1}
, we see that

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{2}\frac{h(y)\overline{\psi}(y)}{v(y)-\lambda}\mathrm{d}y\right|+\left|\int_{0}^{2}\frac{g(y)\overline{\psi}(y)}{(v(y)-\lambda)^{2}}\mathrm{d}y\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{\|h\|_{L^{2}(0,2)}}{c_{0}(\vartheta_{1}-y_{\lambda})}+\frac{\|g\|_{L^{2}(0,2)}}{c_{0}^{2}(\vartheta_{1}-y_{\lambda})^{2}}\right)\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(0,2)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1-\epsilon_{1}}{2}\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(0,2)}+C_{\epsilon_{1},\vartheta_{1},\lambda}\left(\|h\|_{L^{2}(0,2)}+\|g\|_{L^{2}(0,2)}\right)

from which the resolvent estimate (
8.2
) follows. The proof for the case
{\rm Re}(\lambda)\in(v(\vartheta_{2}),v(2)]
follows the same lines. With this, the proposition is established.
∎

We can further exploit H
{\mathcal{P}}
, H
v
and H1 to show that the limiting values
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
with
{\rm Re}(\lambda)=v(\vartheta_{1})
or
{\rm Re}(\lambda)=v(\vartheta_{2})
are not discrete eigenvalues of
L_{k}
.

Lemma 8.3
.

Let
v(y)
and
{\mathcal{P}}(y)
satisfy H
{\mathcal{P}}
, H
v
and H1. Let
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
with
{\rm Re}(\lambda)=v(\vartheta_{1})
or
{\rm Re}(\lambda)=v(\vartheta_{2})
. Then,
\lambda\not\in\sigma_{disc}(L_{k})
.

Proof.

We shall see that there is no non-zero solution
\begin{pmatrix}\psi\\
\rho\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{D}(L_{k})
to

\partial_{y}^{2}\psi(y)-k^{2}\psi(y)-\frac{v^{\prime\prime}(y)}{v(y)-\lambda}\psi(y)+\frac{{\mathcal{P}}(y)}{(v(y)-\lambda)^{2}}\psi(y)=0,\quad\rho(y)=\frac{{\mathcal{P}}(y)\psi(y)}{v(y)-\lambda}

(8.6)

with
\psi(0)=\psi(2)=0
and
{\rm Re}(\lambda)=v(\vartheta_{1})
or
{\rm Re}(\lambda)=v(\vartheta_{2})
. Due to the support assumptions on
v^{\prime\prime}
and
{\mathcal{P}}
, we readily see that
\begin{pmatrix}\psi\\
\rho\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{D}(L_{k})
for any such solution
\begin{pmatrix}\psi\\
\rho\end{pmatrix}
to (
8.6
). Say now that
{\rm Re}(\lambda)=v(\vartheta_{1})
, we easily see that

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{2}\frac{v^{\prime\prime}(y)|\psi(y)|^{2}}{v(y)-v(\vartheta_{1})}\mathrm{d}y\right|+\left|\int_{0}^{2}\frac{{\mathcal{P}}(y)|\psi(y)|^{2}}{(v(y)-v(\vartheta_{1}))^{2}}\mathrm{d}y\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{\|v^{\prime\prime\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,2)}}{c_{0}}+\frac{\|{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,2)}}{2c_{0}^{2}}\right)\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(0,2)}

\displaystyle<\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(0,2)}

and thus the usual energy estimate directly yields that
\psi=0
and thus
\rho=0
.
∎

8.2.
Fine properties of the homogeneous solutions

In this subsection we derive several key properties satisfied by the homogeneous solutions of the Taylor-Goldstein equation under H
{\mathcal{P}}
, H
v
and the spectral assumptions H1–H3, that will be used to rule out the existence of embedded eigenvalues in Propositions
8.12
-
8.14
and the existence eigenvalues with non-zero imaginary part in Proposition
8.15
. They will also be essential for the Limiting Absorption Principles of Propositions
10.1
and
10.2
below.

Assumptions H
{\mathcal{P}}
, H
v
and H1 provide a lower bound control on the homogeneous solution
\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})
introduced in (
7.1
) for
\vartheta_{1}<y_{0}<\vartheta_{1}+\delta
and
\vartheta_{2}-\delta<y_{0}<\vartheta_{2}
, for some
\delta>0
small.

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
k\geq 1
. There exists
\delta>0
such that

\displaystyle\left|\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})\right|\geq\frac{1}{2},

for all
y\in[0,2]
,
y_{0}\in[\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{1}+\delta)\cup(\vartheta_{2}-\delta,\vartheta_{2}]
and
0\leq\varepsilon\leq\delta
.

Proof.

Thanks to the continuity of
\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})
with respect to
y\in[0,2]
,
y_{0}\in[\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2}]
and
\varepsilon\in[0,2]
from Proposition
7.4
, there exists some
\delta>0
such that

\displaystyle\|\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(\cdot,y_{0})-\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}(\cdot,\vartheta_{i})\|_{L^{\infty}(0,2)}\leq\frac{1}{2}

for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\delta)
and all
y_{0}\in[\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{1}+\delta)
or
y_{0}\in(\vartheta_{2}-\delta,\vartheta_{2}]
. Hence, the lemma follows once we show that

\displaystyle\left|\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}(y,\vartheta_{i})\right|\geq 1,

for all
y\in(0,2)
. We now argue for
\vartheta_{1}
and we recall that

\displaystyle\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(y,\vartheta_{1})=\sum_{n\geq 0}\left(k^{2}{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{r}},k,0}^{\pm}\right)^{n}(1)(y,\vartheta_{1}).

In particular, if
{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{r}},k,0}^{\pm}f(y,\vartheta_{1})\geq 0
whenever
f(y,\vartheta_{1})\geq 0
for all
y\in[0,2]
, we then deduce that
\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,0}^{\pm}(y,\vartheta_{1})\geq 1
, for all
y\in[0,2]
. Thus, since

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{r}},k,0}^{\pm}f(y,\vartheta_{1})=\int_{\vartheta_{1}}^{y}\frac{1}{(v(z)-v(\vartheta_{1}))^{2}}\int_{\vartheta_{1}}^{z}\left(k^{2}(v(s)-v(\vartheta_{1}))^{2}-{\mathcal{P}}(s)\right)f(s,\vartheta_{1})\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}z

and

\displaystyle k^{2}(v(s)-v(\vartheta_{1}))^{2}-{\mathcal{P}}(s)\geq c_{0}^{2}(s-\vartheta_{1})^{2}-\frac{\|{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,2)}}{2}(s-\vartheta_{1})^{2}\geq 0

because of
{\mathcal{P}}(\vartheta)={\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}(\vartheta)=0
and H1, we see that
\inf_{y\in[0,2]}{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{r}},k,0}^{\pm}f(y,\vartheta_{1})\geq 0
whenever
\inf_{y\in[0,2]}f(y,\vartheta_{1})\geq 0
and the proof is concluded.
∎

Lemma 8.5
.

Let
v(y)
and
{\mathcal{P}}(y)
satisfy H2. Then, there exists
\delta>0
such that

(1)

If
y_{0}\in\left(\vartheta_{1},\varpi_{1}\right]
, then
\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})\neq 0
, for all
0\leq\varepsilon<\delta
and all
y\in[0,y_{0}]
.

(2)

If
y_{0}\in\left[\varpi_{2},\vartheta_{2}\right)
, then
\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}(y,y_{0})\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})\neq 0
, for all
0\leq\varepsilon<\delta
and all
y\in[y_{0},2]
.

Proof.

We argue for
\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
and for
y_{0}\in[\varpi_{1},\vartheta_{2})
, for which
\gamma_{0}=\mu_{0}\in\left[0,\frac{1}{2}\right)
, the other combinations follow the same ideas. Arguing as in the proof of Lemma
8.4
, since
\gamma_{0}<\frac{1}{2}
we appeal to Proposition
7.4
to deduce that

\displaystyle\|\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(\cdot,y_{0})-\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,0}^{\pm}(\cdot,y_{0})\|_{L^{\infty}(0,2)}<\frac{1}{2}

for all
0\leq\varepsilon<\delta
, for some
\delta>0
. Hence, since

\displaystyle\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})=\sum_{n\geq 0}\left(k^{2}{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{s}},k,0}^{\pm}\right)^{n}(1)(y,y_{0}).

we shall check that

{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}f(y,y_{0})=\int_{y_{0}}^{y}\frac{k^{2}}{(v(z)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon)^{1-2\mu_{0}}}\int_{y_{0}}^{z}F_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(s,y_{0})(v(s)-v(y_{0})\pm i\varepsilon)^{-2\mu_{0}}f(s,y_{0})\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}z\geq 0

for all
y\in[y_{0},2]
whenever
f(y,y_{0})\geq 0
for all
y\in[y_{0},2]
, to conclude that
\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})\neq 0
for all
y\in[y_{0},2]
. Further recalling that

F_{{\mathsf{s}},k,0}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})=(v(y)-v(y_{0}))-\frac{v^{\prime}(y)^{2}}{k^{2}}\frac{{\mathcal{J}}(y)-{\mathcal{J}}(y_{0})}{v(y)-v(y_{0})}+\frac{\frac{1}{2}+\mu_{0}}{k^{2}}v^{\prime\prime}(y),

since
v^{\prime}(y)\geq c_{0}>0
,
{\mathcal{J}}^{\prime}(y)\leq 0
and
v^{\prime\prime}(y)\geq 0
for all
y\in[y_{0},2]\subseteq[\tilde{y},2]
we deduce that
F_{{\mathsf{s}},k,0}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})\geq 0
for all
y\in[y_{0},2]
and thus
\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})\geq 1
for all
y\in[y_{0},2]
.
∎

The following result is a consequence of Lemma
8.5
.

Proposition 8.6
.

Let
k\geq 1
. Let
y_{j}\in I_{W}
and
\varepsilon_{j}>0
such that
\varepsilon_{j}\rightarrow 0
and
y_{j}\rightarrow y_{0}\in I_{W}
such that
{\mathcal{J}}(y_{0})\in(0,\frac{1}{4})
. Then,

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(2,y_{j})\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(0,y_{j})-\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(0,y_{j})\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(2,y_{j})\neq 0.

Proof.

Recalling that

\displaystyle\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(y,y_{j})

\displaystyle=(v(y)-v(y_{j})\pm i\varepsilon_{j})^{\frac{1}{2}+\gamma_{j}}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(y,y_{j}),

\displaystyle\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(y,y_{j})

\displaystyle=(v(y)-v(y_{j})\pm i\varepsilon_{j})^{\frac{1}{2}-\gamma_{j}}\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(y,y_{j}),

since
\varepsilon_{j}>0
and
\phi_{\tau,1,k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(y,y_{j})
is continuous in
\varepsilon_{j}
and
y_{j}
uniformly in
y\in[0,2]
, confer Proposition
7.4
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(0,y_{j})

\displaystyle=-ie^{-i\mu_{0}\pi}(v(y_{0})-v(0))^{\frac{1}{2}+\mu_{0}}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(0,y_{0}),

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(0,y_{j})

\displaystyle=-ie^{i\mu_{0}\pi}(v(y_{0})-v(0))^{\frac{1}{2}-\mu_{0}}\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(0,y_{0}),

and

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(2,y_{j})=(v(2)-v(y_{0}))^{\frac{1}{2}+\mu_{0}}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(2,y_{0}),

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(2,y_{j})=(v(2)-v(y_{0}))^{\frac{1}{2}-\mu_{0}}{\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(2,y_{0})},

Hence,

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}

\displaystyle\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(2,y_{j})\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(0,y_{j})-\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(0,y_{j})\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(2,y_{j})

\displaystyle=-i(v(y_{0})-v(0))^{\frac{1}{2}-\mu_{0}}(v(2)-v(y_{0}))^{\frac{1}{2}-\mu_{0}}\left(e^{-i\mu_{0}\pi}(v(y_{0})-v(0))^{2\mu_{0}}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(0,y_{0}){\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(2,y_{0})}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.-e^{i\mu_{0}\pi}(v(2)-v(y_{0}))^{2\mu_{0}}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(2,y_{0}){\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(0,y_{0})}\right)

For

\displaystyle a_{0}

\displaystyle=(v(y_{0})-v(0))^{2\mu_{0}}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(0,y_{0}){\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(2,y_{0})},

\displaystyle a_{1}

\displaystyle=(v(2)-v(y_{0}))^{2\mu_{0}}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(2,y_{0}){\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(0,y_{0})}

we observe that

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}

\displaystyle\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(2,y_{j})\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(0,y_{j})-\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(0,y_{j})\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(2,y_{j})

\displaystyle=i(v(y_{0})-v(0))^{\frac{1}{2}-\mu_{0}}(v(2)-v(y_{0}))^{\frac{1}{2}-\mu_{0}}\left((a_{0}-a_{1})\cos(\mu_{0}\pi)-i(a_{0}+a_{1})\sin(\mu_{0}\pi)\right).

If the above limit is zero, since
\mu_{0}\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
we have that
\cos(\mu_{0}\pi)\sin(\mu_{0}\pi)\neq 0
so that, together with the monotonicity of
v
, we deduce that we must have
a_{0}=a_{1}=0
. However, assume now that there exists some
y_{0}\in\left(y_{>},\vartheta_{2}\right)
for which
\gamma_{0}\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right)
and
a_{0}=0
. From Lemma
8.5
, since
\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(2,y_{0})\neq 0
, this forces
\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(0,y_{0})=0
. As
\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,0}^{\pm}(\cdot,y_{0})
and
\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,0}^{\pm}(\cdot,y_{0})
are linearly independent solutions (they have non-zero Wronskian), we see that
\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(0,y_{0})\neq 0
. On the other hand, Lemma
8.5
also gives
\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(2,y_{0})\neq 0
and thus we conclude that
a_{1}\neq 0
, and thus the limit is non-zero. This same argument shows that
a_{0}\neq 0
if
a_{1}=0
and it extends to the case where
y_{0}\in(\vartheta_{1},\varpi_{1})
.
∎

We next state a result analogous to Proposition
8.6
, which now addresses the case of strong stratification.

Proposition 8.7
.

Let
k\geq 1
. Let
y_{j}\in I_{W}
and
\varepsilon_{j}>0
such that
\varepsilon_{j}\rightarrow 0
and
y_{j}\rightarrow y_{0}\in I_{S}
such that
{\mathcal{J}}(y_{0})>\frac{1}{4}
. Then,

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(2,y_{j})\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(0,y_{j})-\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(0,y_{j})\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(2,y_{j})\neq 0.

Proof.

We note that as
{\mathcal{J}}(y_{0})>\frac{1}{4}
, there holds
\gamma_{0}=i\nu_{0}
with
\nu_{0}>0
so that
\gamma_{j}=i\nu_{j}
with
\nu_{j}>0
, for
j
large enough. Thanks to the continuity of
\phi_{\tau,1,k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(y,y_{j})
and the relation
\overline{\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\mp}(y,y_{j})}=\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(y,y_{j})
, which is deduced from (
7.1
), (
7.8
) and (
7.9
) we obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(0,y_{j})

\displaystyle=-ie^{\nu_{0}\pi}(v(y_{0})-v(0))^{\frac{1}{2}+i\nu_{0}}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(0,y_{0}),

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(0,y_{0})

\displaystyle=-ie^{-\nu_{0}\pi}(v(y_{0})-v(0))^{\frac{1}{2}-i\nu_{0}}\overline{\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\mp}(0,y_{0})},

and

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(2,y_{j})

\displaystyle=(v(2)-v(y_{0}))^{\frac{1}{2}+i\nu_{0}}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(2,y_{0}),

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(2,y_{0})

\displaystyle=(v(2)-v(y_{0}))^{\frac{1}{2}-i\nu_{0}}\overline{\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\mp}(2,y_{0})}.

Moreover, due to the continuity of
\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(y,y_{j})
with respect to
\varepsilon_{j}>0
, there holds

\displaystyle{\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\mp}(0,y_{0})}={\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(0,y_{0})},\quad{\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\mp}(2,y_{0})}={\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(2,y_{0})}.

Then, we reach

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(2,y_{j})\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(0,y_{j})

\displaystyle-\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(0,y_{j})\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(2,y_{j})

\displaystyle=i(v(y_{0})-v(0))^{\frac{1}{2}}(v(2)-v(y_{0}))^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(e^{\nu_{0}\pi}\zeta-e^{-\nu_{0}\pi}\overline{\zeta}\right)

where

\displaystyle\zeta:=(v(y_{0})-v(0))^{i\nu_{0}}(v(2)-v(y_{0}))^{-i\nu_{0}}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(0,y_{0})\overline{\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(2,y_{0})}.

Since
\nu_{0}>0
, we see that the limit vanishes if and only if
\zeta=0
. Hence, if the limit vanishes then either
\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(0,y_{0})=0
or
\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(2,y_{0})=0
. If
\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(2,y_{0})=0
, from the Wronskian invariance we have

-2\gamma_{0}v^{\prime}(y_{0})=-\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,0}^{\pm}(2,y_{0})\partial_{y}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(2,y_{0})

and thus
\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(2,y_{0})\neq 0
. On the other hand,

\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})=\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(y,y_{j})=\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\overline{\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\mp}(y,y_{j})}=\overline{\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})}=0,

thus reaching a contradiction. A similar contradiction is obtained supposing that
\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(0,y_{0})=0
. As a result,
\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(0,y_{0})\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(2,y_{0})\neq 0
and the corollary follows.
∎

To obtain the analogue of Propositions
8.6
and
8.7
for the mild stratification regime, we first show an intermediate result.

Lemma 8.8
.

Let
k\geq 1
,
y_{j},\,y_{0}\in I_{M}
, with
{\mathcal{J}}(y_{0})=\frac{1}{4}
,
y_{j}\rightarrow y_{0}
and
\varepsilon_{j}\rightarrow 0^{+}
as
j\rightarrow\infty
. Then,

\displaystyle\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(y,y_{0}):=\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(y,y_{j})\in{\mathbb{R}},\quad\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(y,y_{0}):=\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(y,y_{j})\in{\mathbb{R}},

for all
y\in[0,2]
.

Proof.

Since
\phi_{\sigma,1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}:=(I-k^{2}{\mathcal{T}}_{\sigma,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm})^{-1}1
, we see that
\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\phi_{\sigma,1,k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(y,y_{j})\in{\mathbb{R}}
provided that
\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}{\mathcal{T}}_{\sigma,k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(1)(y,y_{j})\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Indeed, recall that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}_{\sigma,k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(1)(y,y_{j})=\int_{y_{j}}^{y}\frac{1}{(v(z)-v(y_{j})\pm i\varepsilon_{j})^{1+\mathbf{s}(\sigma)2\gamma_{j}}}\int_{y_{j}}^{z}\mathrm{F}_{\sigma,k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(s,y_{j})(v(s)-v(y_{j})\pm i\varepsilon_{j})^{\mathbf{s}(\sigma)2\gamma_{j}}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}z,

where
\mathbf{s}(\sigma)=1
for
\sigma={\mathsf{r}}
and
\mathbf{s}(\sigma)=-1
for
\sigma={\mathsf{s}}
. Since
\gamma_{j}\rightarrow 0
as
j\rightarrow\infty
and
\mathrm{F}_{{\mathsf{r}},k,0}^{\pm}(y,y_{0}):=\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{F}_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(y,y_{j})\in{\mathbb{R}}
for all
y\in[0,2]
, the Dominated Convergence Theorem shows that

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}{\mathcal{T}}_{\sigma,k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(1)(y,y_{j})=\int_{y_{0}}^{y}\frac{1}{v(z)-v(y_{0})}\int_{y_{0}}^{z}\mathrm{F}_{\sigma,k,0}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}z:={\mathcal{T}}_{\sigma,k,0}^{\pm}(1)(y,y_{0})\in{\mathbb{R}}

and thus
\phi_{\sigma,k,0}^{\pm}(y,y_{0}):=\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\phi_{\sigma,1,k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(y,y_{j})\in{\mathbb{R}}
, for
\sigma\in\{{\mathsf{r}},{\mathsf{s}}\}
. In particular, this same argument shows that

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}\phi_{k,\varepsilon_{j}}(y,y_{j})\in{\mathbb{R}}

if
\phi_{k,\varepsilon_{j}}(y,y_{j})\in L^{\infty}
uniformly in
y_{j}\in I_{M}
and
\varepsilon_{j}>0
and
\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\phi_{k,\varepsilon_{j}}(y,y_{j})\in{\mathbb{R}}
, for all
y\in[0,2]
. Hence, for the second part of the lemma, thanks to (
7.12
) we just need to see that

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\left(\frac{{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}-T_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}}{2\gamma_{j}}\right)\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{j})\in{\mathbb{R}}

(8.7)

for all
y\in[0,2]
. To that end, we note that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\left(\frac{v(z)-v(y_{j})\pm i\varepsilon_{j}}{v(y)-v(y_{j})\pm i\varepsilon_{j}}\right)^{2\gamma_{j}}-\left(\frac{v(z)-v(y_{j})\pm i\varepsilon_{j}}{v(y)-v(y_{j})\pm i\varepsilon_{j}}\right)^{-2\gamma_{j}}}{2\gamma_{j}}-2\log\left(\frac{v(z)-v(y_{j})\pm i\varepsilon_{j}}{v(y)-v(y_{j})\pm i\varepsilon_{j}}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\lesssim\gamma_{j}^{2}\left|\frac{v(z)-v(y_{j})\pm i\varepsilon_{j}}{v(y)-v(y_{j})\pm i\varepsilon_{j}}\right|^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left|\log^{3}\left|\frac{v(z)-v(y_{j})\pm i\varepsilon_{j}}{v(y)-v(y_{j})\pm i\varepsilon_{j}}\right|\right|

for all
y,z\in[0,2]
, all
y_{j}\in I_{M}
and all
\varepsilon_{j}>0
. In particular, we now have

\displaystyle\left(\frac{{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}-T_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}}{2\gamma_{j}}\right)\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}(y,y_{j})

\displaystyle\qquad=2\int_{y_{j}}^{y}\frac{1}{v(z)-v(y_{j})\pm i\varepsilon_{j}}\int_{y_{j}}^{z}\mathrm{F}_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(s,y_{j})\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(s,y_{j})\log\left(\frac{v(s)-v(y_{j})\pm i\varepsilon_{j}}{v(z)-v(y_{j})\pm i\varepsilon_{j}}\right)\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\qquad-\int_{y_{j}}^{y}\frac{1}{k^{2}(v(z)-v(y_{j})\pm i\varepsilon_{j})}\int_{y_{j}}^{s}v^{\prime\prime}(s)\left(\frac{v(s)-v(y_{j})\pm i\varepsilon_{j}}{v(z)-v(y_{j})\pm i\varepsilon_{j}}\right)^{-2\gamma_{j}}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(s,y_{j})\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\qquad+O(\gamma_{j}^{2})\int_{y_{j}}^{y}\frac{1}{v(z)-v(y_{j})\pm i\varepsilon_{j}}\int_{y_{j}}^{z}\mathrm{F}_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(s,y_{j})\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(s,y_{j})\log^{3}\left(\frac{v(s)-v(y_{j})\pm i\varepsilon_{j}}{v(z)-v(y_{j})\pm i\varepsilon_{j}}\right)\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}z.

The third contribution clearly vanishes in the limit. For the second contribution we can use the Dominated Convergence Theorem directly and show it converges to

\displaystyle\int_{y_{0}}^{y}\frac{1}{k^{2}(v(z)-v(y_{0}))}\int_{y_{0}}^{z}v^{\prime\prime}(s)\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(s,y_{0})\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}z\in{\mathbb{R}}

while for the first contribution we note that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{v(z)-v(y_{j})\pm i\varepsilon_{j}}\int_{y_{j}}^{z}\mathrm{F}_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(s,y_{j})\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(s,y_{j})\log\left(\frac{v(s)-v(y_{j})\pm i\varepsilon_{j}}{v(z)-v(y_{j})\pm i\varepsilon_{j}}\right)\mathrm{d}s\right|

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\frac{1}{|v(z)-v(y_{j})\pm i\varepsilon_{j}|}\int_{y_{j}}^{z}\left|\frac{v(s)-v(y_{j})\pm i\varepsilon_{j}}{v(z)-v(y_{j})\pm i\varepsilon_{j}}\right|^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim 1,

and thus we can use the Dominated Convergence Theorem as well to show that its limit is

\displaystyle 2\int_{y_{0}}^{y}\frac{1}{v(z)-v(y_{0})}\int_{y_{0}}^{z}\mathrm{F}_{{\mathsf{r}},k,0}^{\pm}(s,y_{0})\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(s,y_{0})\log\left(\frac{v(s)-v(y_{0})}{v(z)-v(y_{0})}\right)\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}z\in{\mathbb{R}}.

With this we conclude that (
8.7
) holds and the lemma follows.
∎

We are now in position to present the analogue of Propositions
8.6
and
8.7
in the mildly stratified region.

Proposition 8.9
.

Let
k\geq 1
,
y_{j}\rightarrow y_{0}
with
{\mathcal{J}}(y_{0})=\frac{1}{4}
,
{\mathcal{J}}(y_{j})\neq\frac{1}{4}
and
\varepsilon_{j}\rightarrow 0^{+}
as
j\rightarrow\infty
. Then,

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(0,y_{j})\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(2,y_{j})-\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(2,y_{j})\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(0,y_{j})\neq 0.

Proof.

From Proposition
7.4
and Lemma
8.8
we have that

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(0,y_{j})=\pm i(v(y_{0})-v(0))^{\frac{1}{2}}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}(0,y_{0}),

and

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(2,y_{j})=(v(2)-v(y_{0}))^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\log(v(2)-v(y_{0}))\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(2,y_{0})+\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(2,y_{0})\right)

while

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(2,y_{j})=(v(2)-v(y_{0}))^{\frac{1}{2}}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(2,y_{0})

and

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(0,y_{j})=\pm i(v(y_{0})-v(0))^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\left(\log(v(y_{0})-v(0))\pm i\pi\right)\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(0,y_{0})+\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(0,y_{0})\right)

Therefore, we have that

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}

\displaystyle\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(0,y_{j})\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(2,y_{j})-\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(2,y_{j})\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(0,y_{j})

\displaystyle=\pi(v(2)-v(y_{0}))^{\frac{1}{2}}(v(y_{0})-v(0))^{\frac{1}{2}}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(0,y_{0})\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(2,y_{0})

\displaystyle\quad\pm i(v(y_{0})-v(0))^{\frac{1}{2}}(v(2)-v(y_{0}))^{\frac{1}{2}}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}(0,y_{0})\left(\log(v(2)-v(y_{0}))\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(2,y_{0})+\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(2,y_{0})\right)

\displaystyle\quad\mp i(v(y_{0})-v(0))^{\frac{1}{2}}(v(2)-v(y_{0}))^{\frac{1}{2}}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}(2,y_{0})\left(\log(v(2)-v(y_{0}))\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(0,y_{0})+\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(0,y_{0})\right)

with further
\phi_{\sigma,1,k,0}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})\in{\mathbb{R}}
for all
y\in[0,2]
and for
\sigma\in\{{\mathsf{r}},{\mathrm{L}}\}
. Assume now that
y_{0}=\varpi_{2}
, so that
\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(2,y_{0})\geq 1
due to Lemma
8.5
. Then, if
\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(0,y_{0})\neq 0
we see that the limit has non-zero real part. Instead, if
\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(0,y_{0})=0
, since
{\mathcal{W}}\{\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,0}^{\pm},\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},k,0}^{\pm}\}=v^{\prime}(y_{0})\neq 0
, we deduce that
\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(0,y_{0})\neq 0
and

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}

\displaystyle\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(0,y_{j})\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(2,y_{j})-\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(2,y_{j})\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(0,y_{j})

\displaystyle=\mp i(v(y_{0})-v(0))^{\frac{1}{2}}(v(2)-v(y_{0}))^{\frac{1}{2}}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}(2,y_{0})\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(0,y_{0})\neq 0.

For
y_{0}=\varpi_{1}
we argue similarly, now observing that
\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}^{\pm}(0,y_{0})\neq 0
. We omit the details.
∎

We shall need later in Proposition
8.15
a precise description of the limiting homogeneous solutions for the critical interphase
y_{0}=\varpi_{1}
and
y_{0}=\varpi_{2}
. Arguing as in Lemma
8.8
we now have

Lemma 8.10
.

Let
k\geq 1
,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{j}\rightarrow\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}>0
,
y_{j}\rightarrow y_{0}=\tilde{\varpi}_{1}
or
y_{0}=\tilde{\varpi}_{2}
, with
{\mathfrak{g}}_{j}\mathrm{P}(y_{j})=\frac{1}{4}
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}\mathrm{P}(\tilde{\varpi}_{n})=\frac{1}{4}
and
\varepsilon_{j}>0
with
\varepsilon_{j}\rightarrow 0
as
j\rightarrow\infty
. Then,

\displaystyle\phi_{rL,1,k,0}^{\pm}(y,y_{0}):=\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(y,y_{j})\in{\mathbb{R}},

for all
y\in[0,2]
.

Proof.

Since
{\mathfrak{g}}_{j}\mathrm{P}(y_{j})=\frac{1}{4}
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}\mathrm{P}(\tilde{\varpi}_{n})=\frac{1}{4}
for all
j\geq 1
, we shall use (
7.14
). From (
7.15
) we have that

\displaystyle\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})=\left(1-k^{2}{\mathcal{T}}_{0,k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}\right)^{-1}\left(k^{2}{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathrm{L}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}\phi_{0,1,k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}\right)(y,y_{0}).

As
\varepsilon_{j}\rightarrow 0
, we see that
{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathrm{L}},k,0}:=\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathrm{L}},k,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
given by (
7.16
) is a real valued operator acting on real functions. Since
\phi_{0,1,k,0}(y,y_{0})\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
{\mathcal{T}}_{0,k,0}
is a real valued operator acting on real functions due to Lemma
8.8
, we deduce that
\phi_{rL,1,k,0}(y,y_{0})\in{\mathbb{R}}
as well.
∎

With the above lemma at hand, we next obtain

Proposition 8.11
.

Let
k\geq 1
,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{j}\rightarrow\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}>0
,
y_{j}\rightarrow y_{0}=\tilde{\varpi}_{1}
or
y_{0}=\tilde{\varpi}_{2}
, with
{\mathfrak{g}}_{j}\mathrm{P}(y_{j})=\frac{1}{4}
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}\mathrm{P}(\tilde{\varpi}_{n})=\frac{1}{4}
and
\varepsilon_{j}>0
with
\varepsilon_{j}\rightarrow 0
as
j\rightarrow\infty
. Then,

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(0,y_{j})\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(2,y_{j})-\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(2,y_{j})\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(0,y_{j})\neq 0.

Proof.

Let
C_{v}:=(v(2)-v(y_{0}))^{\frac{1}{2}}(v(y_{0})-v(0))^{\frac{1}{2}}>0
. Due to Proposition
7.6
, a routine computation shows that for
\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(\cdot,y_{j})
given by (
7.14
), we have

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}

\displaystyle\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(0,y_{j})\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(2,y_{j})-\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(2,y_{j})\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(0,y_{j})

\displaystyle=C_{v}\pi\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}(0,y_{0})\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}(2,y_{0})

\displaystyle\quad\pm C_{v}i\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}(0,y_{0})\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}(2,y_{0})\log\left(\frac{v(2)-v(y_{0})}{v(y_{0})-v(0)}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\pm C_{v}i\left(\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}(0,y_{0})\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,0}(2,y_{0})-\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}(2,y_{0})\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,0}(0,y_{0})\right).

Hence, if
\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}(0,y_{0})\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon}(2,y_{0})\neq 0
then the limit has non-zero real part, and it is thus non-zero. Assume next that
\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}(0,y_{0})\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}(2,y_{0})=0
and assume further that
y_{0}=\widetilde{\varpi}_{2}
, so that
\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}(2,y_{0})\neq 0
due to Lemma
8.5
and thus
\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}(0,y_{0})=0
. Since
{\mathcal{W}}\{\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,0}^{\pm},\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},k,0}^{\pm}\}=v^{\prime}(y_{0})\neq 0
, we have
\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,0}(0,y_{0})\neq 0
and thus

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}

\displaystyle\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(0,y_{j})\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(2,y_{j})-\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(2,y_{j})\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{\pm}(0,y_{j})=\mp C_{v}i\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}(2,y_{0})\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,0}(0,y_{0})\neq 0.

For
y_{0}=\widetilde{\varpi}_{1}
we now have
\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}(0,y_{0})\neq 0
and we argue similarly, we omit the details.
∎

8.3.
Absence of embedded eigenvalues

In this section we show that
\lambda=v(y_{0})
is not an embedded eigenvalue of
L_{k}
, for all
y_{0}\in(\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2})
. In the following three propositions we assume as usual that H
{\mathcal{P}}
-H
v
and H1–H3 hold.

Proposition 8.12
.

Let
y_{0}\in(\vartheta_{1},\varpi_{1})\cup(\varpi_{2},\vartheta_{2})
. Then
\lambda=v(y_{0})
is not an embedded eigenvalue of
L_{k}
.

Proof.

Assume without loss of generality that
y_{0}\in(\varpi_{2},\vartheta_{2})
and that there exists a non-zero solution
\psi\in H^{2}\cap H^{1}_{0}
to (
8.6
), with
\lambda=v(y_{0})
so that
\gamma_{0}=\mu_{0}\in(0,\frac{1}{4})
. For
y>y_{0}
we observe that

\displaystyle\psi(y)=C\left(\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,0}(2,y_{0})\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,0}(y,y_{0})-\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,0}(2,y_{0})\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,0}(y,y_{0})\right)

for some
C>0
and where we recall that

\displaystyle\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,0}(y,y_{0})

\displaystyle=(v(y)-v(y_{0}))^{\frac{1}{2}+\mu_{0}}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}(y,y_{0}),

\displaystyle\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,0}(y,y_{0})

\displaystyle=(v(y)-v(y_{0}))^{\frac{1}{2}-\mu_{0}}\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,0}(y,y_{0}),

with
\phi_{\sigma,1,k,0}(y_{0},y_{0})=1
and
\left|\partial_{y}\phi_{\sigma,1,k,0}(y,y_{0})\right|\lesssim 1
, confer Propositions
7.2
and
7.4
. We shall now see that
\psi(y)\not\in H^{1}(y_{0},2)
. To that purpose, we note that

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(y)

\displaystyle=Cv^{\prime}(y_{0})\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,0}(2,y_{0})\left(\frac{1}{2}+\mu_{0}\right)(v(y)-v(y_{0}))^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mu_{0}}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}(y,y_{0})

\displaystyle\quad-Cv^{\prime}(y_{0})\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,0}(2,y_{0})\left(\frac{1}{2}-\mu_{0}\right)(v(y)-v(y_{0}))^{-\frac{1}{2}-\mu_{0}}\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,0}(y,y_{0})

\displaystyle\quad+C\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,0}(2,y_{0})(v(y)-v(y_{0}))^{\frac{1}{2}+\mu_{0}}\partial_{y}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}(y,y_{0})

\displaystyle\quad-C\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,0}(2,y_{0})(v(y)-v(y_{0}))^{\frac{1}{2}-\mu_{0}}\partial_{y}\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},1,k,0}(y,y_{0})

It is immediate to see that the last two contributions are in
H^{1}(y_{0},2)
and so we focus on the first two. Further recalling that
\phi_{\sigma,1,k,0}(y_{0},y_{0})=1
and
\left|\partial_{y}\phi_{\sigma,1,k,0}(y,y_{0})\right|\lesssim 1
, we shall see that

\displaystyle(v(y)-v(y_{0}))^{-\frac{1}{2}-\mu_{0}}\left(\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,0}(2,y_{0})\left(\frac{1}{2}+\mu_{0}\right)(v(y)-v(y_{0}))^{2\mu_{0}}-\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,0}(2,y_{0})\left(\frac{1}{2}-\mu_{0}\right)\right)\not\in L^{2}(y_{0},2).

Indeed, since
\mu_{0}\neq\frac{1}{2}
and also
\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,0}(2,y_{0})\neq 0
, confer Lemma
8.5
, we have that

\displaystyle\left|\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,0}(2,y_{0})\left(\frac{1}{2}+\mu_{0}\right)(v(y)-v(y_{0}))^{2\mu_{0}}\right|\leq\frac{1}{2}\left|\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,0}(2,y_{0})\left(\frac{1}{2}-\mu_{0}\right)\right|

(8.8)

for
y>y_{0}
sufficiently close to
y_{0}
. Moreover, since
(v(y)-v(y_{0}))^{-\frac{1}{2}-\mu_{0}}\not\in L^{2}(y_{0},2)
we conclude that
\psi^{\prime}(y)\not\in L^{2}(y_{0},2)
.
∎

We next argue for the mild regime.

Proposition 8.13
.

Let
y_{0}=\varpi_{1}
or
y_{0}=\varpi_{2}
. Then,
\lambda=v(y_{0})
is not an embedded eigenvalue of
L_{k}
.

Proof.

Assume
y_{0}=\varpi_{2}
, with
\gamma_{0}=0
. Appealing to the homogeneous solutions constructed in
7.2
and
7.6
, we now have

\displaystyle\psi(y)=C\left(\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,0}(2,y_{0})\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},k,0}(y,y_{0})-\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},k,0}(2,y_{0})\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,0}(y,y_{0})\right),

where we recall

\displaystyle\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},k,0}(y,y_{0})

\displaystyle=(v(y)-v(y_{0}))^{\frac{1}{2}}\log(v(y)-v(y_{0}))\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}(y,y_{0})

\displaystyle\quad+(v(y)-v(y_{0}))^{\frac{1}{2}}\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,0}(y,y_{0}),

with further
\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,0}(y_{0},y_{0})=1
and
\left|\partial_{y}\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,0}(y,y_{0})\right|\lesssim 1
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(y)

\displaystyle=\frac{C}{2}v^{\prime}(y)(v(y)-v(y_{0}))^{-\frac{1}{2}}(v(2)-v(y_{0}))^{\frac{1}{2}}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}(2,y_{0})\log\left(\frac{v(y)-v(y_{0})}{v(2)-v(y_{0})}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+Cv^{\prime}(y)(v(y)-v(y_{0}))^{-\frac{1}{2}}(v(2)-v(y_{0}))^{\frac{1}{2}}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}(2,y_{0})

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{C}{2}v^{\prime}(y)(v(y)-v(y_{0}))^{-\frac{1}{2}}(v(2)-v(y_{0}))^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\phi_{{\mathrm{L}},1,k,0}(2,y_{0})-\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}(2,y_{0})\right)+\widetilde{\psi}(y)

with
\widetilde{\psi}\in L^{2}(0,2)
. For
y>y_{0}
close enough, we see from the above that the most singular factor is

\displaystyle v^{\prime}(y)(v(y)-v(y_{0}))^{-\frac{1}{2}}(v(2)-v(y_{0}))^{\frac{1}{2}}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}(2,y_{0})\log(v(y)-v(y_{0}))\not\in L^{2}(y_{0},2)

since
v^{\prime}(y)\geq c_{0}>0
and
\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0}(2,y_{0})\neq 0
due to Lemma
8.5
. With this, we see that
\psi^{\prime}(y)\not\in L^{2}(y_{0},2)
.
∎

We last show there are no embedded eigenvalues in the strongly stratified region.

Proposition 8.14
.

Let
y_{0}\in(\varpi_{1},\varpi_{2})
. Then
\lambda=v(y_{0})
is not an embedded eigenvalue of
L_{k}
.

Proof.

For
y_{0}\in(\varpi_{1},\varpi_{2})
we now have
\gamma_{0}=i\nu_{0}
with
\nu_{0}>0
. Arguing as in the proof of Proposition
8.12
, we shall now see that

\displaystyle\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,0}(2,y_{0})

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{2}+i\nu_{0}\right)(v(y)-v(y_{0}))^{-\frac{1}{2}+i\nu_{0}}

\displaystyle\quad-\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,0}(2,y_{0})\left(\frac{1}{2}-i\nu_{0}\right)(v(y)-v(y_{0}))^{-\frac{1}{2}-i\nu_{0}}\not\in L^{2}(y_{0},2).

Since
\overline{\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,0}(2,y_{0})}=\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,0}(2,y_{0})\neq 0
, we must show that

\displaystyle(v(y)-v(y_{0}))^{-\frac{1}{2}}{\rm Im}\left(\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,0}(2,y_{0})\left(\frac{1}{2}+i\nu_{0}\right)(v(y)-v(y_{0}))^{i\nu_{0}}\right)\not\in L^{2}(y_{0},2).

To do so, we note that
\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,0}(2,y_{0})\left(\tfrac{1}{2}+i\nu_{0}\right)=re^{i\alpha}
, for some
r>0
and
\alpha\in[0,2\pi)
. Hence,

\displaystyle\left|{\rm Im}\left(\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,0}(2,y_{0})\left(\frac{1}{2}+i\nu_{0}\right)(v(y)-v(y_{0}))^{i\nu_{0}}\right)\right|=r\left|\sin(\alpha+\nu_{0}\log(v(y)-v(y_{0})))\right|

Hence, with the change of variables
x=v(y)-v(y_{0})
, noting that
v^{\prime}(y)\geq c_{0}>0
we shall show that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2}x^{-1}\sin^{2}(\alpha+\nu_{0}\log(x))\mathrm{d}x=+\infty.

We next note that

\displaystyle\left|\sin(\alpha+\nu_{0}\log(x))\right|^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}

for all
x>0
such that
\alpha+\nu_{0}\log(v(y)-v(y_{0}))\in(\frac{\pi}{4}-2\pi k,\frac{3\pi}{4}-2\pi k)
, namely for

all
x\in(e^{\nu_{0}^{-1}(\frac{\pi}{4}-2\pi k-\alpha)},e^{\nu_{0}^{-1}(\frac{3\pi}{4}-2\pi k-\alpha)})
, for all
k\geq 1
. In particular,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2}x^{-1}\sin^{2}(\alpha+\nu_{0}\log(x))\mathrm{d}x\geq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\geq 1}^{\infty}\int_{e^{\nu_{0}^{-1}(\frac{\pi}{4}-2\pi k-\alpha)}}^{e^{\nu_{0}^{-1}(\frac{3\pi}{4}-2\pi k-\alpha)}}x^{-1}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\geq 1}^{\infty}\log(x)\Big|_{x=e^{\nu_{0}^{-1}(\frac{\pi}{4}-2\pi k-\alpha)}}^{x=e^{\nu_{0}^{-1}(\frac{3\pi}{4}-2\pi k-\alpha)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{k\geq 1}^{\infty}\frac{\pi}{\nu_{0}}=+\infty.

With this, we conclude that
\psi^{\prime}(y)\not\in L^{2}(y_{0},2)
.
∎

8.4.
Absence of imaginary spectrum

To conclude the proof of Theorem
2.4
, it only remains to see there are no eigenvalues with non-zero imaginary part.

Proposition 8.15
.

Let
v(y)
and
{\mathcal{P}}(y)
be such that H
{\mathcal{P}}
, H
v
and H1–H3 hold. Then,
\lambda\not\in\sigma(L_{k})
, for all
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
with
{\rm Im}(\lambda)\neq 0
.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}\in[0,{\mathfrak{g}}]
. Let
\lambda=\lambda_{r}+i\lambda_{i}
, with
\lambda_{i}\neq 0
and
\lambda_{r}\in[v(0),v(2)]
. Since
\lambda_{i}\neq 0
, by classical ODE theory we note that
\lambda\in\sigma(L_{k})
if and only if
\lambda
is a discrete eigenvalue of
L_{k}
. Hence, we shall see that there is no non-zero solution
\psi
to

\left(\partial_{y}^{2}-k^{2}-\frac{v^{\prime\prime}(y)}{v(y)-\lambda}+\frac{\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}\mathrm{P}(y)}{(v(y)-\lambda)^{2}}\right)\psi=0,\quad\psi(0)=\psi(2)=0

(8.9)

for all
\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}\in[0,{\mathfrak{g}}]
. We begin by remarking that for
\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}=0
, the Taylor-Goldstein equation reduces to the Rayleigh equation

\left(\partial_{y}^{2}-k^{2}-\frac{v^{\prime\prime}(y)}{v(y)-\lambda}\right)\psi=0,\quad\psi(0)=\psi(2)=0,

(8.10)

which has no non-zero solution, thanks to the assumption H3 on the absence of eigenvalues of the linearised Euler operator. Let
\varphi(y,\lambda,{\mathfrak{g}})
be the unique solution to

\left(\partial_{y}^{2}-k^{2}-\frac{v^{\prime\prime}(y)}{v(y)-\lambda}+\frac{\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}\mathrm{P}(y)}{(v(y)-\lambda)^{2}}\right)\psi=0,\quad\psi(2)=0,\quad\psi^{\prime}(2)=1,

(8.11)

so that
\varphi(y,\lambda,{\mathfrak{g}})
is an eigenfunction associated to the eigenvalue
\lambda
provided that
\varphi(0,\lambda,{\mathfrak{g}})=0
. Hence, we define the Wronskian
{\mathcal{W}}(\lambda,{\mathfrak{g}})=\varphi(0,\lambda,{\mathfrak{g}})
, which is continuous in
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus[v(0),v(2)])
. Set also

\mathfrak{G}=\{\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}\in[0,{\mathfrak{g}}]:\,\exists\lambda\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{R}:\mathcal{W}(\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}},\lambda)=0\},

(8.12)

where
\mathcal{R}=[v(0),v(2)]
is the range of the shear flow
v(y)
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}=\left\{\lambda=\lambda_{r}+i\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\lambda_{r}\in[v(0),v(2)],\quad\lambda_{i}\in[0,2]\right\}

For all
\lambda\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{R}
, we have
{\rm Im}(\lambda)\neq 0
and thus the solution
\varphi(y,\lambda,{\mathfrak{g}})
is classical and
C^{2}
smooth. We shall see that
\mathfrak{G}
is both open and closed in the subset topology of
[0,{\mathfrak{g}}]
with respect to
{\mathbb{R}}
, a connected space. Hence, it is either
[0,{\mathfrak{g}}]
or the empty set. Since
0\not\in\mathfrak{G}
because of H3, we conclude that
\mathfrak{G}
is empty.

Firstly, to see that
\mathfrak{G}
is open, assume otherwise and let
\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}\in\mathfrak{G}
such that for all
\delta>0
there exists some
\mathfrak{h}\in B_{\delta}(\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}})
with
\mathfrak{h}\not\in\mathfrak{G}
. Let
\lambda_{0}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{R}
be such that
\mathcal{W}(\lambda_{0},\tilde{\mathfrak{g}})=0
. Since
\lambda_{0}
is an isolated zero of
{\mathcal{W}}(\lambda,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}})
, which is analytic in
\lambda\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{R}
, there exists some
\delta_{1}>0
sufficiently small such that for
U_{\delta_{1}}(\lambda_{0})=\{\lambda\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{R}:\lambda\in B_{\delta_{1}}(\lambda_{0})\}
, there holds
\mathcal{W}(\lambda,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}})\neq 0
, for all
\lambda\in\partial U_{\delta_{1}}(\lambda_{0})
. Set
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{2}\min_{\lambda\in\partial U_{\delta_{1}}(\lambda_{0})}|\mathcal{W}(\lambda,{\mathfrak{g}})|>0
. Since
\mathcal{W}(\lambda,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}})
is continuous in both
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\mathcal{R}
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}\geq 0
, there exists some
\delta_{2}>0
such that

|\mathcal{W}(\lambda,\mathfrak{h})-\mathcal{W}(\lambda,\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}})|<\varepsilon<|\mathcal{W}(\lambda,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}})|,

(8.13)

for all
|\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}-\mathfrak{h}|<\delta_{2}
. Now, by Rouche’s Theorem, the number of zeroes of
\mathcal{W}(\lambda,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}})
in
U_{\delta_{1}}(\lambda_{0})
(which is one,
\lambda_{0}
) coincides with the number of zeroes of
\mathcal{W}(\lambda,\mathfrak{h})
in
U_{\delta_{1}}(\lambda_{0})
, which is 0 by assumption! Thus we reach a contradiction and conclude that
\mathfrak{G}
is open.

On the other hand, in order to show that
\mathfrak{G}
is closed, let
(\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{j})_{j\geq 1}\subset\mathfrak{G}
and assume that
\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}_{j}}\rightarrow\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we shall see that
\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}\in\mathfrak{G}
. Note that for each
\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{j}
there exists some
\lambda_{j}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{R}
such that

\varphi(0,\lambda_{j},\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{j})=\mathcal{W}(\lambda_{j},\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{j})=0

(8.14)

for all
j\geq 1
. Moreover, up to a subsequence,
\lambda_{j}\rightarrow\lambda\in\mathcal{S}
. If
\lambda\not\in\mathcal{R}
, since
\mathcal{W}
is continuous at
(\lambda,\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}})\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{R}\times[0,{\mathfrak{g}}]
and in a small neighbourhood of it, there holds
\mathcal{W}(\lambda,\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}})=0
, and thus
\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}\in\mathfrak{G}
, it is closed. We shall now argue that we cannot have
\lambda\in\mathcal{R}
.

\bullet

Case
\lambda\in\mathcal{R}
and
\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}>0
.
We shall see this scenario cannot take place. If
\lambda_{j}\rightarrow\lambda\in\mathcal{R}\subset{\mathbb{R}}
, we have that
{\rm Im}(\lambda_{j})\rightarrow 0
. Moreover, Proposition
8.2
shows that
\lambda\in[v(\vartheta_{1}),v(\vartheta_{2})]
. We next distinguish
\lambda
according to its stratified strength and its approximating sequence. In what follows, let
y_{j}\in[\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2}]
be such that
v(y_{j})={\rm Re}(\lambda_{j})
and
\varepsilon_{j}={\rm Im}(\lambda_{j})>0
, so that
v(y_{0})=\lambda
and
\varepsilon_{j}\rightarrow 0
. Due to H2, for all
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{j}>0
, the set
\{y\in[0,2]:\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{j}\tilde{\mathcal{J}}(y)=\frac{1}{4}\}
has at most two connected components. Each connected component is either a point or a closed interval. We shall assume here that they are points, and we comment afterwards the main modifications for the interval case. Hence, let
\widetilde{\varpi}_{j}
denote the unique solution if
\max_{y\in[0,2]}\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{j}\tilde{\mathcal{J}}=\frac{1}{4}
and
\widetilde{\varpi}_{j,1}<\widetilde{\varpi}_{j,2}
denote the two distinct solutions if
\max_{y\in[0,2]}\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{j}\tilde{\mathcal{J}}>\frac{1}{4}
. Likewise, we define
\widetilde{\varpi}
and
\widetilde{\varpi}_{1}<\widetilde{\varpi}_{2}
the solutions associated to the equation
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}(y)=\frac{1}{4}
.

\bullet

Case
y_{0}=\vartheta_{n}
and
y_{j}=\vartheta_{n}
, for
n=1,2
.
We have that
\mathcal{W}(\lambda_{j},\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{j})=\varphi(0,\lambda_{j},\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{j})=0
. Since
{\rm Re}(\lambda_{j})=v(\vartheta_{n})
for all
j\geq 1
and
\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{j}\leq{\mathfrak{g}}
, we see with Lemma
8.3
that
\varphi(0,\lambda_{j},\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{j})=0
forces
\varphi(y,\lambda_{j},\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{j})
to be identically zero, thus
\lambda_{j}
is not an eigenvalue, a contradiction with
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{j}\in\mathfrak{G}
.

\bullet

Case
y_{0}=\vartheta_{n}
and
{\mathcal{P}}(y_{j})>0
.
Then, for
j
large enough we have from Lemma
8.4
that

|\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon_{j}}^{+}(y,y_{j})|\geq\frac{1}{2}
for all
y\in[0,2]
and thus

\displaystyle\varphi(y,\lambda_{j},\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{j})=\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j},\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{j}}^{+}(y,y_{j})\int_{0}^{y}\frac{1}{(\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j},\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{j}}^{+}(z,y_{j}))^{2}}\mathrm{d}z-\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j},\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{j}}^{+}(y,y_{j})\int_{0}^{2}\frac{1}{(\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j},\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{j}}^{+}(z,y_{j}))^{2}}\mathrm{d}z

is the unique well-defined solution to

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{y}-k^{2}-\frac{v^{\prime\prime}(y)}{v(y)-v(y_{j})\pm i\varepsilon_{j}}+\frac{\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{j}\mathrm{P}(y)}{(v(y)-v(y_{j})\pm i\varepsilon_{j})^{2}}\right)\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j},\tilde{g}_{j}}^{+}(y,y_{0})=0.

with
\varphi(2,\lambda_{j},\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{j})=0
and
\partial_{y}\varphi(2,\lambda_{j},\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{j})=1
. Thanks to Proposition
7.4
we next note that

\displaystyle\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0,\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}}^{\pm}(y,y_{0})=\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,\varepsilon_{j},\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{j}}^{+}(y,y_{j})

satisfies

\displaystyle\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0,\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}}^{+}(y,y_{0})=1+k^{2}\int_{y_{0}}^{y}\frac{1}{(v(z)-v(y_{0}))^{2}}\int_{y_{0}}^{z}\mathrm{F}_{{\mathsf{r}},k,0,\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}}^{+}(s,y_{0})(v(s)-v(y_{0}))\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0,\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}}^{+}(s,y_{0})\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}z

so that
\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,0,\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}}(y,y_{0}):=(v(y)-v(y_{0}))\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},1,k,0,\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}}^{+}(y,y_{0})
satisfies

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{y}-k^{2}-\frac{v^{\prime\prime}(y)}{v(y)-v(y_{0})}+\frac{\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}\mathrm{P}(y)}{(v(y)-v(y_{0}))^{2}}\right)\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,0,\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}}^{+}(y,y_{0})=0.

Moreover, we have from Proposition
9.4
and Lemma
9.9
that for
\varphi(y,\lambda,\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}})=\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\varphi(y,\lambda_{j},\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{j})
we have

\displaystyle\varphi(y,\lambda,\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}})=\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,0,\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}}^{+}(y,y_{0})\int_{0}^{y}\frac{1}{(\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,0,\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}}^{+}(z,y_{0}))^{2}}\mathrm{d}z-\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,0,\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}}^{+}(y,y_{0})\int_{0}^{2}\frac{1}{(\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,0,\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}}^{+}(z,y_{0}))^{2}}\mathrm{d}z

is well-defined and, moreover, it satisfies

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{y}-k^{2}-\frac{v^{\prime\prime}(y)}{v(y)-v(y_{0})}+\frac{\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}\mathrm{P}(y)}{(v(y)-v(y_{0}))^{2}}\right)\varphi(y,\lambda,\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}})=0

with
\varphi(2,\lambda,\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}})=0
. In particular, we also have
\varphi(0,\lambda,\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}})=0
. However, Proposition
9.4
and
\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,0,\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}}^{+}(y,y_{0})\neq 0
for all
y\in[0,2]\setminus\{y_{0}\}
due to Lemma
8.4
show that
\varphi(0,\lambda,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}})\neq 0
, reaching a contradiction.

\bullet

Case
y_{0}\in(\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2})
and
\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}(y_{0})\neq\frac{1}{4}
.
We now have that
y_{j}=v^{-1}({\rm Re}(\lambda_{j}))
is such that
\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{j}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}(y_{j})\neq\frac{1}{4}
and thus
\gamma_{j}\not\rightarrow 0
, for
j\geq 1
large enough and thus

\varphi(y,\lambda_{j},\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{j})=\frac{\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon_{j},\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{j}}^{+}(2,y_{j})\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j},\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{j}}^{+}(y,y_{j})-\phi_{{\mathsf{r}},k,\varepsilon_{j},\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{j}}^{+}(2,y_{j})\phi_{{\mathsf{s}},k,\varepsilon_{j},\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{j}}^{+}(y,y_{j})}{2\gamma_{j}v^{\prime}(y_{j})}

(8.15)

solves (
8.11
) with
\varphi(2,\lambda_{j},\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{j})=0
and
\partial_{y}\varphi(2,\lambda_{j},\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{j})=1
. If
\widetilde{\varpi}_{1}<\widetilde{\varpi}_{2}
,
Proposition
8.7
for
y_{0}\in(\widetilde{\varpi}_{1},\widetilde{\varpi}_{2})
and Proposition
8.6
for
y_{0}\in(\vartheta_{1},\widetilde{\varpi}_{1})\cup(\widetilde{\varpi}_{2},\vartheta_{2})
, show that
|\varphi(0,\lambda_{j},\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{j})|\geq c_{0}>0
, for some
c_{0}>0
, for all
j
large enough, thus contradicting (
8.14
). If
\widetilde{\varpi}
is the unique root to
